of this report.]

\subsection{Cigler's moments and the normalization}
A binomial coefficient is zero outside its ordinary range. Following
Cigler \cite{Cigler}, define
\begin{equation}
 c_0(t)=1,\qquad
 c_r(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}
 \binom{\lfloor(r-1)/2\rfloor}{\lfloor(j-1)/2\rfloor}
 \binom{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}t^{j-1}
 \quad(r\ge1).
 \label{cp:eq:moments}
\end{equation}
For instance, $c_1=1$, $c_2=1+t$, $c_3=1+t+t^2$, and
$c_4=1+2t+2t^2+t^3$. Put
\begin{equation}
 D_k(n;t)=\det(c_{k+i+j}(t))_{0\le i,j<n},\qquad D_k(0;t)=1.
 \label{cp:eq:hankel}
\end{equation}
Throughout the article $k\ge1$ and
\begin{equation}
 a=\lfloor k/2\rfloor,\qquad b=\lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor,
 \qquad M=k-1,\qquad X_k(t)=(\rep1a,t,\rep{t^2}b).
 \label{cp:eq:alphabet}
\end{equation}
Here $\rep{x}{r}$ denotes $r$ repetitions of $x$, not an exponent.
The explicit interface supplied by Part~II (Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:schur}) is
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad
 \Ht_k(n;t):=s_{(n^a)}(X_k(t))
 =(-1)^{k\binom n2}t^{-\binom n2}D_k(n;t).
 \quad}
 \label{cp:eq:interface}
\end{equation}
The identity means that the quotient extends to the polynomial on the left,
including at $t=0$. It is not permission to evaluate an undefined quotient.
The empty-height Schur polynomial gives $\Ht_1(n;t)=1$.

Our spectral theorems are unconditional statements about the explicitly
defined Schur sequence on the left of \eqref{cp:eq:interface}. Their application
to the original Hankel determinants uses exactly the displayed identity.
The repository proves that identity through a Laurent-moment parity
factorization and a Toeplitz factorization; it is not assumed merely because
finite numerical values agree. For the fourth-root analysis we also use its
odd-shift parity identity, which is rederived in Section~\ref{cp:sec:quartic}.

\subsection{What is new here, and what is background?}
The principal addition is the all-$k$, all-$\ell$ minimality theorem in
Section~\ref{cp:sec:main}. Two useful intermediate results are the general
centered subleading-coefficient formula in Section~\ref{cp:sec:jet} and the
four explicit fourth-root product formulas in Section~\ref{cp:sec:quartic}.
The five-coefficient calculation in Section~\ref{cp:sec:triple} proves, rather
than extrapolates, the exceptional loss of three pole orders.

Schur polynomials, the bialternant formula, confluence of repeated variables,
Christoffel transformations, and beta-moment Hankel determinants are existing
mathematics \cite{GSM,Kratt}. We supply the particular calculations needed
here, and do not present these general techniques as discoveries. The
repository's Schur identity and generic leading-coefficient theorem are
likewise not claimed as additions of this article.

A bounded literature check included Cigler's original paper, repeated-variable
bialternant work \cite{GSM}, determinant-calculus sources \cite{Kratt}, and
Chern--Shi's August 2026 paper \cite{ChernShi}. The latter treats shifted
Catalan-like arrays indexed by a fixed meander height, a different parameter
family from the three-cluster rectangular character studied here. No matching
all-shift cyclotomic pole classification was identified in that check. This is
a description of the check, not a proof of worldwide priority.

\section{The exact cyclotomic classification}
\label{cp:sec:main}

\subsection{Minimal recurrence and pole multiplicity}
Let $E$ be the forward shift: $(Ef)(n)=f(n+1)$. A monic polynomial $R(E)$
is a constant-coefficient recurrence if $R(E)f(n)=0$ for every $n\ge0$.
The minimal one divides every such recurrence. The denominator of
\begin{equation}
 \GG_k(z,t)=\sum_{n\ge0}\Ht_k(n;t)z^n
 \label{cp:eq:gf}
\end{equation}
is always normalized to have constant term one and to be relatively prime
to its numerator. An exponent zero means that a factor is omitted.

For $0\le q\le M$, define
\begin{equation}
 d_q=\left\lfloor\frac{q(M-q)}2\right\rfloor.
 \label{cp:eq:dq}
\end{equation}
At a primitive $\ell$th root $\zeta$, $\ell\ge3$, define the baseline exponents
\begin{equation}
 e^0_\rho=
 \begin{cases}
 1+\max\{d_q:0\le q\le M,\ q\equiv\rho\pmod\ell\},
      &\text{if this set is nonempty},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
 \quad 0\le\rho<\ell.
 \label{cp:eq:baseline}
\end{equation}
They use a \emph{maximum}, not a sum. The following theorem gives the exact
correction to that maximum.

\begin{theorem}[Complete cyclotomic pole classification]
\label{cp:thm:main}
Let $k\ge1$ and let $\zeta$ have exact order $\ell\ge3$.
Start with $e_\rho=e^0_\rho$ from \eqref{cp:eq:baseline}.

If $\ell>M$ or $\ell\not\equiv M\pmod2$, no correction is required.
Otherwise put
\begin{equation}
 q_*=(M-\ell)/2,\quad \rho_*=q_*\bmod\ell,\quad
 D=\left\lfloor\frac{M^2-\ell^2}{8}\right\rfloor.
 \label{cp:eq:tie}
\end{equation}
Only $e_{\rho_*}$ can change. The cancellation criterion is
\begin{equation}
 \begin{array}{c|c}
 k=2a & a+D\text{ is odd}\\
 k=2a+1 & D\text{ is even}.
 \end{array}
 \label{cp:eq:criterion}
\end{equation}
If the criterion fails, leave $e_{\rho_*}=D+1$. If it holds, set
\begin{equation}
 e_{\rho_*}=
 \begin{cases}
 \max(0,D-2),&\ell=4\text{ and }k\equiv1\pmod4,\\
 D,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{cp:eq:correction}
\end{equation}
Then the reduced denominator and the monic minimal recurrence are exactly
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \QQ_{k,\zeta}(z)=\prod_{\rho=0}^{\ell-1}(1-\zeta^\rho z)^{e_\rho},
 \qquad R_{k,\zeta}(E)=\prod_{\rho=0}^{\ell-1}(E-\zeta^\rho)^{e_\rho}.}
 \label{cp:eq:answer}
\end{equation}
The exponent list depends on $k$ and $\ell$, not on the choice of primitive
root of that order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[What ``loss of three'' means]
In the exceptional family write $k=4m+1$, $m\ge1$. Then
$D=2m^2-2$. For $m\ge2$ the exponent drops from $D+1$ to $D-2$,
exactly three orders. For $m=1$, the baseline exponent is one and the
factor disappears; negative exponents are not introduced.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Small examples}
The exponent vectors below are ordered by phases
$1,\zeta,\ldots,\zeta^{\ell-1}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrl@{}}
\toprule
$k$ & $\ell$ & baseline $e^0$ & exact $e$ & outcome\\
\midrule
$4$ & $3$ & $(1,2,2)$ & $(1,2,2)$ & tie, no cancellation\\
$5$ & $4$ & $(1,2,3,2)$ & $(0,2,3,2)$ & disappearance\\
$6$ & $3$ & $(4,3,4)$ & $(4,2,4)$ & one-order loss\\
$6$ & $5$ & $(1,3,4,4,3)$ & $(0,3,4,4,3)$ & disappearance\\
$7$ & $4$ & $(5,3,5,5)$ & $(5,2,5,5)$ & one-order loss\\
$9$ & $4$ & $(9,8,7,8)$ & $(9,8,4,8)$ & three-order loss\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \QQ_{9,\ii}(z)=(1-z)^9(1+z)^4(1+z^2)^8.
 \label{cp:eq:k9}
\end{equation}
The maximum-degree rule alone would incorrectly give exponent seven at
$1+z$. Direct substitution into the generic denominator would be still
less accurate because it would add all colliding multiplicities.

\subsection{Outline of the proof}
The proof has three separable parts. First, strict concavity shows that only
one residue can have two maximal-degree contributions. Second, a negative-index
reflection identity decides whether their leading terms cancel. The explicit
first subleading coefficient proves that cancellation stops immediately,
except at primitive fourth roots in one congruence class of $k$.
Third, exact fourth-root product formulas show that two further coefficients
cancel there, and that the next permitted coefficient is nonzero.
Each ingredient is proved in the following sections.

\section{Confluent rectangular characters and their spectral terms}
\label{cp:sec:confluence}

\subsection{The distinct-variable subset identity}
For $K$ distinct nonzero variables $x_1,\ldots,x_K$, a rectangle of height
$A$, and $C=K-A$, the bialternant gives
\begin{equation}
 s_{(n^A)}(X)
 =\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}\\ |I|=A}}
 \left(\prod_{i\in I}x_i\right)^n
 \prod_{\substack{i\in I\\j\notin I}}(1-x_j/x_i)^{-1}.
 \label{cp:eq:subset}
\end{equation}
Indeed, use exponent columns
$0,1,\ldots,C-1,n+C,\ldots,n+K-1$, and expand along the last $A$
columns. The Vandermonde factors internal to $I$ and its complement cancel.
The cross factors and the powers $x_i^C$ give precisely
\eqref{cp:eq:subset}. This also defines an exponential-polynomial extension
$\widetilde s_A(n;X)$ for all integer $n$; no negative partition is being
asserted to exist.

Now coalesce $m_i$ variables at distinct nonzero nodes $\alpha_i$,
$1\le i\le L$, where $\sum_i m_i=K$. An allocation is a vector
$\boldsymbol r$ with $0\le r_i\le m_i$ and $\sum_i r_i=A$. Write
\begin{equation}
 s_i=m_i-r_i,\quad d_i=r_is_i,\quad d=\sum_i d_i,\quad
 \lambda_{\boldsymbol r}=\prod_i\alpha_i^{r_i},\quad
 B(m,r)=\frac{\prod_{h=0}^{r-1}h!}{\prod_{h=m-r}^{m-1}h!}.
 \label{cp:eq:clusterdata}
\end{equation}
Empty products equal one, in particular $B(m,0)=B(m,m)=1$.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical cluster polynomials]
\label{cp:lem:cluster}
There are canonically grouped polynomials $P_{\boldsymbol r}(n)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 s_{(n^A)}(\rep{\alpha_1}{m_1},\ldots,\rep{\alpha_L}{m_L})
 =\sum_{\boldsymbol r}\lambda_{\boldsymbol r}^{\,n}P_{\boldsymbol r}(n).
 \label{cp:eq:clusters}
\end{equation}
They have exact degree $d$ and nonzero leading coefficient
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 L_{\boldsymbol r}=\prod_i B(m_i,r_i)
 \prod_{i\ne j}(1-\alpha_j/\alpha_i)^{-r_i s_j}.}
 \label{cp:eq:leading}
\end{equation}
The grouping is by allocations, even when two products
$\lambda_{\boldsymbol r}$ coincide.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The normalized confluent row of order $h$ at $\alpha_i$ in an exponent
column $e$ is $\binom eh\alpha_i^{e-h}$. The denominator is
$\prod_{i<j}(\alpha_j-\alpha_i)^{m_i m_j}$. Expand the numerator along
its $A$ high columns. Selecting $r_i$ rows in cluster $i$ extracts the
factor $\alpha_i^{nr_i}$, so the remaining coefficient is polynomial
in $n$.

Suppose the selected derivative orders in this cluster form $R_i$.
The normalized Leibniz rule for $x^n g(x)$ gives a degree bound
$\sum_{h\in R_i}h-\binom{r_i}{2}$: distinct derivative rows of $g$
are required in a nonzero determinant, and their smallest possible total
order is $\binom{r_i}{2}$. The unique maximizing selection is
$R_i=\{s_i,\ldots,m_i-1\}$, giving degree $r_is_i$.
Its local leading determinant is
\begin{equation}
 \det\left(\frac1{(s_i+h-p)!}\right)_{0\le h,p<r_i}=B(m_i,r_i),
 \label{cp:eq:factorial}
\end{equation}
where negative factorials mean zero entries. Multiply row $h$ by
$(s_i+h)!$; the columns become monic falling-factorial polynomials at
$s_i,s_i+1,\ldots,s_i+r_i-1$, proving \eqref{cp:eq:factorial}.

The remaining low and high confluent Vandermondes give
\[
 (-1)^{\sum_{i<j}r_i s_j}
 \frac{\prod_i B(m_i,r_i)\alpha_i^{Cr_i-r_is_i}}
 {\prod_{i<j}(\alpha_j-\alpha_i)^{s_i r_j+r_i s_j}},
\]
which is \eqref{cp:eq:leading}. All factors are nonzero. This proof is the
confluent leading-term argument already used in Part~II
(Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:cluster}); general
repeated-variable bialternants are treated in \cite{GSM}.
\end{proof}

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch 66: Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:cluster} is Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:cluster}, whose nodes $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_\ell$ are
written $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_L$ here, and \eqref{cp:eq:leading} is
\eqref{rs:eq:LC}, written with normalized cross factors; the proof above
condenses Part~II's argument, as it says. Part~II's remark after that
theorem already notes that summands with a common product must be
combined; Sections~\ref{cp:sec:reflection}--\ref{cp:sec:triple} carry
out that combination at roots of unity.]

\subsection{Specialization to the three-cluster alphabet}
For $t\ne0,\pm1$, the nodes $1,t,t^2$ are distinct. In
\eqref{cp:eq:clusterdata} take multiplicities $(a,1,b)$ and height $A=a$.
There is exactly one allocation for each $q=0,\ldots,M$:
\begin{equation}
 r_0=a-\lceil q/2\rceil,\qquad r_1=q\bmod2,\qquad r_2=\lfloor q/2\rfloor.
 \label{cp:eq:allocation}
\end{equation}
Its eigenvalue is $t^q$. Direct substitution gives degree $d_q$ in
\eqref{cp:eq:dq}. Hence
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_k(n;t)=\sum_{q=0}^{M}t^{qn}P_{k,q}(n;t),\qquad
 \deg_nP_{k,q}=d_q.
 \label{cp:eq:spectral}
\end{equation}
Each canonical $P_{k,q}$ is a rational function of $t$ with polynomial
coefficients in $n$; its only possible parameter poles are $0,1,-1$.
Thus \eqref{cp:eq:spectral} specializes regularly at every root of unity
of order at least three. Confluence of \emph{nodes} is not needed there:
only eigenvalue products collide.

\begin{lemma}[Minimality for exponential polynomials]
\label{cp:lem:minimality}
If $f(n)=\sum_\lambda\lambda^n A_\lambda(n)$, with distinct nonzero
$\lambda$ and nonzero polynomials $A_\lambda$, then its minimal recurrence
is $\prod_\lambda(E-\lambda)^{1+\deg A_\lambda}$.
Its reduced generating denominator is
$\prod_\lambda(1-\lambda z)^{1+\deg A_\lambda}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The operator $E-\lambda$ lowers the polynomial degree in the $\lambda$
mode by one, whereas $E-\mu$, $\mu\ne\lambda$, preserves it with
nonzero leading multiplier $\lambda-\mu$. Applying operators that kill
all but one mode proves their linear independence and the stated
minimality. Equivalently, a degree-$d$ polynomial times $\lambda^n$
has a pole of exact order $d+1$ at $z=\lambda^{-1}$.
\end{proof}
For a root $\zeta$, therefore, the entire question is the exact degree of
\begin{equation}
 A_\rho(n)=\sum_{q\equiv\rho\ (\mathrm{mod}\ \ell)}P_{k,q}(n;\zeta).
 \label{cp:eq:grouped}
\end{equation}
If this polynomial is zero, its phase is absent.

\section{Why there is at most one cancellation class}
\label{cp:sec:reflection}

\subsection{The concavity restriction}
\begin{lemma}[Unique possible tie]
\label{cp:lem:tie}
Among the $q$ in a fixed residue class modulo $\ell\ge3$, the maximum
of $d_q$ is unique except possibly for a pair $q,M-q$.
A two-way maximum occurs in exactly one residue if and only if
$\ell\le M$ and $\ell\equiv M\pmod2$. Its two indices are
$q_*$ and $q_*+\ell$ from \eqref{cp:eq:tie}.
Every other index in that residue, when present, has degree at most
$D-\ell^2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $d_q=d_p$, the integers $q(M-q)$ and $p(M-p)$ differ by at most one.
Their difference is $(q-p)(M-q-p)$. For different indices in one residue,
$|q-p|\ge\ell\ge3$, so necessarily $q+p=M$.
If two symmetric maxima were separated by more than $\ell$, an intermediate
index of the same residue would be strictly closer to $M/2$ and have larger
degree. Thus $p-q=\ell$, proving the criterion and uniqueness.
The next possible indices are at distance $3\ell/2$ from $M/2$, rather
than $\ell/2$. The quadratic values divided by two decrease by exactly
$\ell^2$; taking floors preserves this integral difference.
\end{proof}

A unique maximum cannot cancel because its leading coefficient in
\eqref{cp:eq:leading} is nonzero. The lemma already proves all uncorrected
entries of Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}.

\subsection{Reflection about the canonical center}
\begin{lemma}[Negative-index reflection]
\label{cp:lem:reflection}
The canonical polynomials of \eqref{cp:eq:spectral} satisfy
\begin{align}
 P_{k,M-q}(n;t)&=(-1)^a t^{a(M-2q)}P_{k,q}(-n-k;t),&&k=2a,
 \label{cp:eq:reflecteven}\\
 P_{k,M-q}(n;t)&=t^{k(a-q)}P_{k,q}(-n-k;t),&&k=2a+1.
 \label{cp:eq:reflectodd}
\end{align}
These are identities of rational functions in $t$ and polynomials in $n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Complementing the subsets in \eqref{cp:eq:subset} gives
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde s_A(-n-K;X)=(-1)^{A(K-A)}
 (\prod X)^{-n-A}s_{(n^{K-A})}(X).
 \label{cp:eq:negativegeneral}
\end{equation}
For example, each cross denominator changes sign on complementation;
there are $A(K-A)$ such signs. This argument works for the
exponential-polynomial extension, so there is no appeal to a Schur
polynomial indexed by a negative partition.

For even $k$, $K-A=A=a$ and $\prod X_k=t^M$. Comparing distinct
powers $t^{qn}$ over $\Q(t)$ in \eqref{cp:eq:negativegeneral} gives
\eqref{cp:eq:reflecteven}. For odd $k$, $\prod X_k=t^k$ and
$X_k^{-1}=t^{-2}X_k$ as multisets. The rectangular complement identity
$s_{(n^{K-A})}(X)=(\prod X)^n s_{(n^A)}(X^{-1})$ therefore yields
$s_{(n^{a+1})}(X_k)=t^n s_{(n^a)}(X_k)$.
Equation~\eqref{cp:eq:reflectodd} follows. The identities continue to every
$t\ne0,\pm1$ because the canonical coefficients are regular there.
\end{proof}

At a tied residue let $q=q_*$ and introduce the centered variable
\begin{equation}x=n+k/2.\label{cp:eq:center}\end{equation}
If $p(x)=P_{k,q}(x-k/2;\zeta)$, the sum of the two maximal-degree
polynomials is
\begin{equation}
 p(x)+\varepsilon p(-x),\qquad
 \varepsilon=
 \begin{cases}(-1)^a,&k\text{ even},\\-1,&k\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{cp:eq:paritypair}
\end{equation}
For the odd case, $a-q_*=\ell/2$ and
$\zeta^{k\ell/2}=(-1)^k=-1$. Its leading coefficient is multiplied by
$1+\varepsilon(-1)^D$, giving exactly \eqref{cp:eq:criterion}.
If $D=0$ and the criterion holds, the constant pair disappears, and there
are no lower terms in that residue by Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:tie}. We henceforth
consider $D>0$.

\section{A general first subleading coefficient for confluent Schur modes}
\label{cp:sec:jet}
The leading coefficient alone cannot distinguish an ordinary cancellation
from the fourth-root exception. We now calculate one further coefficient
for arbitrary cluster multiplicities. This calculation is useful beyond
Cigler's family.

\begin{theorem}[Centered first-coefficient formula]
\label{cp:thm:jet}
Use the notation of Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:cluster}, and suppose $d\ge1$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 P_{\boldsymbol r}(x-K/2)=L_{\boldsymbol r}
 \bigl(x^d+c_{\boldsymbol r}x^{d-1}+O(x^{d-2})\bigr),
 \label{cp:eq:jet}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 c_{\boldsymbol r}=\frac12\sum_{i<j}
 \bigl[d_i(2r_j-m_j)-d_j(2r_i-m_i)\bigr]
 \frac{\alpha_i+\alpha_j}{\alpha_i-\alpha_j}.}
 \label{cp:eq:jetc}
\end{equation}
Here $O(x^u)$ denotes a polynomial of degree at most $u$; if $u<0$,
that polynomial is zero. Equivalently, before centering,
\begin{equation}
 [n^{d-1}]P_{\boldsymbol r}(n)=L_{\boldsymbol r}\sum_i d_i B_i,
 \quad
 B_i=\frac{m_i}{2}+
 \sum_{j\ne i}\frac{r_j-s_j\alpha_j/\alpha_i}{1-\alpha_j/\alpha_i}.
 \label{cp:eq:uncenteredjet}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Split the $i$th cluster formally into
$\alpha_i\exp(\epsilon y_{i,1}),\ldots,
\alpha_i\exp(\epsilon y_{i,m_i})$, with
$\sum_u y_{i,u}=0$. The $y$'s may be chosen distinct and generic.
In \eqref{cp:eq:subset}, group subsets that select $r_i$ variables in each
cluster. Such a grouped expression has a removable singularity as
$\epsilon\to0$: antisymmetrizing within each cluster cancels its
internal Vandermonde, while the cross-cluster denominators remain nonzero.
Its limit is exactly the canonical $P_{\boldsymbol r}(n)$ times
$\lambda_{\boldsymbol r}^n$.

For a selected set $I_i$ let $h_i=\sum_{u\in I_i}y_{i,u}$ and
$h=\sum_i h_i$. Consider the finite alternant functional
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J(f)=
 \sum_{|I_i|=r_i\ \forall i}
 \frac{f(I_1,\ldots,I_L)}
 {\displaystyle\prod_i\prod_{u\in I_i,\ v\notin I_i}(y_{i,u}-y_{i,v})}.
 \label{cp:eq:functional}
\end{equation}
For a polynomial separately symmetric in selected and unselected variables,
possible poles cancel in pairs under an interchange of two variables.
Thus the result is polynomial and symmetric in each whole cluster. If the
input is homogeneous of degree less than $d$, its output is zero; at degree
$d$ it is a constant. This also follows directly by multiplying by the
cluster Vandermondes: the alternating numerator must be divisible by each
of them.

The functional factors by cluster. The same leading confluent determinant
\eqref{cp:eq:factorial}, applied to the exponential subset sum, gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal J\left(\frac{h^d}{d!}\right)=\prod_i B(m_i,r_i),\qquad
 \mathcal J\left(\frac{h_i h^{d-1}}{(d-1)!}\right)
 =d_i\prod_j B(m_j,r_j).
 \label{cp:eq:functionalvalues}
\end{equation}
For clarity, the one-cluster identity in the first formula is
$\mathcal J_i(h_i^{d_i}/d_i!)=B(m_i,r_i)$; it is the coefficient of
$n^{d_i}$ in the coalescing bialternant. In the product functional every
cluster must receive degree at least $d_i$. At total degree $d$, it
receives exactly that degree. The multinomial theorem proves both displayed
formulas, with multiplier $d_i$ in the second. This also covers $d_i=0$.

For selected $u$ and unselected $v$ in one cluster,
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{1-e^{\epsilon(v-u)}}
 =\frac1{\epsilon(u-v)}
 \left(1+\frac\epsilon2(u-v)+O(\epsilon^2)\right).
 \label{cp:eq:internalexpand}
\end{equation}
The total linear correction in cluster $i$ is $m_i h_i/2$ because
$\sum_{u\in I_i,v\notin I_i}(y_{i,u}-y_{i,v})=m_i h_i$.
For two different clusters put $z_{ij}=\alpha_j/\alpha_i$. Their
cross factor expands as
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{1-z_{ij}e^{\epsilon(v-u)}}
 =\frac1{1-z_{ij}}
 \left(1+\epsilon\frac{z_{ij}(v-u)}{1-z_{ij}}+O(\epsilon^2)\right).
 \label{cp:eq:externalexpand}
\end{equation}
Summing $v-u$ over selected variables in $i$ and unselected variables in
$j$ gives $-r_i h_j-s_j h_i$. After both orientations of each pair
are collected, the coefficient of $h_i$ is exactly $B_i$ in
\eqref{cp:eq:uncenteredjet}. The exponential factor contributes
$e^{\epsilon n h}$.

After extracting $\epsilon^{-d}$ and the constant cross-factor product,
the coefficient of $n^d$ in the finite limit comes from $h^d/d!$.
The coefficient of $n^{d-1}$ comes only from the linear correction
$\sum_i B_i h_i$ times $h^{d-1}/(d-1)!$.
Equation~\eqref{cp:eq:functionalvalues} proves \eqref{cp:eq:uncenteredjet}.
No higher-order expansion term can contribute to $n^{d-1}$.

Replacing $n$ by $x-K/2$ subtracts $dK/2$ from the normalized
subleading coefficient. Finally,
\[
 B_i-K/2=\frac12\sum_{j\ne i}(2r_j-m_j)
 \frac{\alpha_i+\alpha_j}{\alpha_i-\alpha_j}.
\]
Antisymmetrization in $i,j$ yields \eqref{cp:eq:jetc}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[A useful consistency check]
For a single cluster the sum in \eqref{cp:eq:jetc} is empty. Thus a rectangular
Weyl-dimension polynomial is centered at $-K/2$ with zero first subleading
coefficient. The formula detects the additional asymmetry introduced by
separated nodes, rather than an arbitrary choice of origin.
\end{remark}

\section{Ordinary cyclotomic cancellations stop after one order}
\label{cp:sec:ordinary}
Set
\begin{equation}
 U(t)=\frac{1+t}{1-t},\qquad V(t)=\frac{1+t^2}{1-t^2}.
 \label{cp:eq:UV}
\end{equation}
For the allocation \eqref{cp:eq:allocation}, put $q=2r+\sigma$,
$\sigma\in\{0,1\}$, and
\[
 d_0=(a-r-\sigma)(r+\sigma),\quad d_2=r(b-r),\quad
 u_0=a-2r-2\sigma,\quad u_2=2r-b.
\]
The middle cluster has $d_1=0$. Equation~\eqref{cp:eq:jetc} becomes
\begin{equation}
 c_q(t)=\frac12\bigl[(2\sigma-1)(d_0-d_2)U(t)
 +(d_0u_2-d_2u_0)V(t)\bigr].
 \label{cp:eq:cq}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Nonvanishing of the centered coefficient]
\label{cp:lem:nonzero}
Suppose a tied maximal pair has common degree $D>0$ and satisfies the
cancellation criterion \eqref{cp:eq:criterion}. Then $c_q(\zeta)\ne0$,
except when $\ell=4$ and $k\equiv1\pmod4$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $\zeta=e^{\ii\theta}$. We repeatedly use
\begin{equation}
 \frac{V(\zeta)}{U(\zeta)}=\frac{\cos\theta}{1+\cos\theta}.
 \label{cp:eq:cosratio}
\end{equation}
All denominators here are legitimate because $\ell\ge3$.
If the cosine of the argument of a root of unity is rational, then
$2\cos\theta=\zeta+\zeta^{-1}$ is a rational algebraic integer and
hence an integer. The only nonreal possibilities are $-1/2,0,1/2$,
corresponding to orders $3,4,6$.

\paragraph{Even $k=2a$.}
Here $M$ and the tied separation $\ell$ are odd. Choose the even index
$q=2r$ in the symmetric pair, and let $h=M-2q=\pm\ell$.
Then $D=r(M-q)$, and \eqref{cp:eq:cq} simplifies to
\begin{equation}
 c_q=-\frac r2 U+\frac{r-Dh}{4}V.
 \label{cp:eq:ceven}
\end{equation}
Since $D>0$, $r>0$. Vanishing would imply
$\cos\theta=-2r/(r+Dh)$; a zero denominator cannot solve the equation.
The order is odd, so the only possibility is $\ell=3$ and cosine
$-1/2$, giving $Dh=3r$. If $h=-3$ this contradicts positivity.
If $h=3$, it would require $M-q=1$, whereas
$M-q=q+3\ge3$. Thus $c_q$ cannot vanish.

\paragraph{Odd $k=2a+1$, even tied index.}
Take the smaller tied index $q=q_*$ and put $v=a-q=\ell/2>0$.
When $q$ is even, \eqref{cp:eq:cq} reduces to
\begin{equation}
 c_q=-\frac{Dv}{2}V.
 \label{cp:eq:coddeven}
\end{equation}
It vanishes exactly when $\zeta^2=-1$, that is, $\ell=4$.
Then $q=a-2$ is even, so $a$ is even and $k\equiv1\pmod4$.

\paragraph{Odd $k=2a+1$, odd tied index.}
For odd $q$, the corresponding simplification is
\begin{equation}
 c_q=\frac v2\bigl(U-(D+1)V\bigr).
 \label{cp:eq:coddodd}
\end{equation}
Vanishing would force $\cos\theta=1/D$. The only admissible rational
cosine is $1/2$, hence $\ell=6$, $v=3$, and $D=2$.
But $D=\lfloor(a^2-9)/2\rfloor=2$ would give
$13\le a^2<15$, impossible for an integer $a$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[One-order cancellation outside the exceptional family]
\label{cp:prop:ordinary}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:nonzero}, excluding its exception,
$\deg A_{\rho_*}=D-1$. Thus the exact exponent is $D$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In \eqref{cp:eq:paritypair}, cancellation of the degree-$D$ term means
$\varepsilon(-1)^D=-1$. The degree-$(D-1)$ coefficient is therefore
$2L_q c_q$, which is nonzero by Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:nonzero}.
All remaining terms in the residue have degree at most $D-\ell^2<D-1$.
They cannot alter it. The $D=0$ case was dealt with immediately after
\eqref{cp:eq:paritypair}.
\end{proof}

\section{Exact fourth-root product formulas}
\label{cp:sec:quartic}
The remaining family is $k=4m+1$, $m\ge1$, at $t=\ii$ or $-\ii$.
We first evaluate its four residue polynomials completely.

Define, for $s\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 \alpha_s(L)&=\prod_{1\le u\le v\le s-1}
       \frac{2L+u+v-1}{u+v-1},\label{cp:eq:alpha}\\
 \beta_s(L)&=\prod_{1\le u\le v\le s-1}
       \frac{2L+u+v}{u+v}.\label{cp:eq:beta}
\end{align}
They are polynomials in $L$ with rational coefficients; $\alpha_1=\beta_1=1$.

\begin{theorem}[Fourth-root quasipolynomials]
\label{cp:thm:quartic}
For all integers $m\ge1$ and $L\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \Ht_{4m+1}(4L;\ii)
 &=\alpha_{m+1}(L)\beta_m(L)^2\beta_{m+1}(L),
 \label{cp:eq:quart0}\\
 \Ht_{4m+1}(4L+1;\ii)
 &=(-1)^m2\alpha_m(L+1)\beta_m(L)\beta_{m+1}(L)^2,
 \label{cp:eq:quart1}\\
 \Ht_{4m+1}(4L+2;\ii)
 &=2\alpha_m(L+1)\beta_m(L+1)\beta_{m+1}(L)^2,
 \label{cp:eq:quart2}\\
 \Ht_{4m+1}(4L+3;\ii)
 &=(-1)^m\alpha_{m+1}(L+1)\beta_m(L+1)^2\beta_{m+1}(L).
 \label{cp:eq:quart3}
\end{align}
The same values hold at $-\ii$. Each residue polynomial has degree
$\delta=2m^2$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The odd-shift parity factorization}
Here is the specific Hankel reduction used in the proof. Put
\[
 Q_t(z)=(1+tz)(1+t/z)=1+t^2+t(z+z^{-1}),\quad x=z+z^{-1},
\]
and, for a symmetric Laurent polynomial $q(z)=q(z^{-1})$, write
$q_j=[z^j]q(z)$ and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal U_N(q)=\det(q_{i-j}+q_{i+j+1})_{i,j<N},\qquad
 \mathcal V_N(q)=\det(q_{i-j}-q_{i+j+2})_{i,j<N}.
 \label{cp:eq:gramUV}
\end{equation}
Both empty determinants are one. They equal the polynomial moment
Gram determinants with weights $q(x)(x+2)/2$ and $q(x)(4-x^2)/2$ under
$\CT_z$. To check this, use the monic bases
\[
 P_j(x)=\frac{z^{j+1}+z^{-j}}{z+1},\qquad
 R_j(x)=\frac{z^{j+1}-z^{-j-1}}{z-z^{-1}}.
\]
Both satisfy the monic recurrence $F_{j+1}=xF_j-F_{j-1}$.
Multiplication gives the entries in \eqref{cp:eq:gramUV}; replacing these
monic bases by powers of $x$ does not change the determinants.

Write $U_h(N)=\mathcal U_N(Q_t^h)$ and
$V_h(N)=\mathcal V_N(Q_t^h)$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_{2h+1}(2N;t)=U_h(N)V_h(N),\qquad
 \Ht_{2h+1}(2N+1;t)=U_h(N+1)V_h(N).
 \label{cp:eq:oddparity}
\end{equation}
For completeness, these identities follow directly from
\eqref{cp:eq:moments}. Let
$u_j=\CT(1+z)Q_t^j$ and $v_j=\CT(1-z^2)Q_t^j$.
Binomial expansion gives $c_{2j+1}=u_j$, $c_{2j+2}=(1+t)u_j$ and
$(1+t)^2u_j-u_{j+1}=t v_j$. Reorder a Hankel matrix of odd shift with
even indices first and subtract $(1+t)$ times each corresponding even row
from the odd row. Its diagonal blocks are a $u$-moment block and a
$-t v$-moment block. Since $Q_t=1+t^2+tx$, changing powers of $Q_t$
to powers of $x$ in a size-$N$ Gram determinant contributes
$t^{N(N-1)}$. For even order the total power is
$N+2N(N-1)=\binom{2N}{2}$; for odd order it is
$N+N(N+1)+N(N-1)=\binom{2N+1}{2}$.
The signs also cancel the normalization in \eqref{cp:eq:interface}.
This proves \eqref{cp:eq:oddparity}; it is the odd part of the repository's
parity factorization, not an independent new claim.

\subsection{Binomial and Catalan moment determinants}
Let $C_j=\binom{2j}{j}/(j+1)$ and, for $s\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}
 A_s(L)=\det\left(\binom{2(s+i+j)}{s+i+j}\right)_{i,j<L},\qquad
 T_s(L)=\det(C_{s+i+j})_{i,j<L}.
 \label{cp:eq:AT}
\end{equation}
The following elementary evaluations will also specify every normalization
in the calculation:
\begin{equation}
 A_s(L)=2^L\alpha_s(L),\qquad T_s(L)=\beta_s(L),\qquad
 \frac{T_s(L)}{A_s(L)}=
 \frac{2^L\rise{1/2}{L}}{\rise{s+L}{L}}.
 \label{cp:eq:ATeval}
\end{equation}
Here $\rise{u}{L}=u(u+1)\cdots(u+L-1)$. A proof from beta moments,
including an elementary determinant proof rather than an appeal to a
Selberg integral, is given in Appendix~\ref{cp:app:beta}.

\subsection{Evaluation of the two Gram factors at a fourth root}
At $t=\ii$, $Q_t=\ii x$. For $h=2m$, remove the scalar
$(-1)^{mN}$ from both size-$N$ Gram determinants, and denote the remaining
ones by $U_N^0,V_N^0$. Their weights are respectively
$x^{2m}(x+2)/2$ and $x^{2m}(4-x^2)/2$.
Because $\CT x^{2j}=\binom{2j}{j}$ and odd moments vanish,
\[
 \tfrac12\CT x^{2j}(4-x^2)=C_j.
\]
Separating even and odd monomials therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 V_{2L}^0=T_m(L)T_{m+1}(L),\qquad
 V_{2L+1}^0=T_m(L+1)T_{m+1}(L).
 \label{cp:eq:V0}
\end{equation}

Let $D_N^0$ be the moment determinant for the even weight $x^{2m}$
alone, and let $p_N(x)$ be its monic orthogonal polynomial. The
Christoffel determinant identity at $-2$, together with
$p_N(-x)=(-1)^Np_N(x)$, gives
\begin{equation}
 U_N^0=2^{-N}D_N^0 p_N(2).
 \label{cp:eq:Christoffel}
\end{equation}
Under $y=x^2/4$, the even polynomials correspond to beta weight
$y^{m-1/2}(1-y)^{-1/2}$ and the odd polynomials to
$y^{m+1/2}(1-y)^{-1/2}$. The endpoint ratio of beta determinants gives
\begin{equation}
 p_{2L}(2)=\frac{4^L\rise{1/2}{L}}{\rise{m+L}{L}},\qquad
 p_{2L+1}(2)=\frac{2\cdot4^L\rise{1/2}{L}}{\rise{m+L+1}{L}}.
 \label{cp:eq:endpoints}
\end{equation}
For instance, a monic polynomial $\pi_L$ for beta weight with parameters
$(A,B)$ satisfies $\pi_L(1)=\rise B L/\rise{A+B+L-1}L$;
this follows by applying the Christoffel identity to $(1-y)$ and taking
the quotient of the two beta determinants in Appendix~\ref{cp:app:beta}.
Thus no unspecified Jacobi-polynomial normalization is used.

Even--odd separation gives
$D_{2L}^0=A_m(L)A_{m+1}(L)$ and
$D_{2L+1}^0=A_m(L+1)A_{m+1}(L)$. Combining
\eqref{cp:eq:ATeval}--\eqref{cp:eq:endpoints} yields
\begin{equation}
 U_{2L}^0=2^{-L}T_m(L)A_{m+1}(L),\qquad
 U_{2L+1}^0=2^{-L}A_m(L+1)T_{m+1}(L).
 \label{cp:eq:U0}
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:quartic}]
Insert \eqref{cp:eq:U0} and \eqref{cp:eq:V0} into \eqref{cp:eq:oddparity},
with $h=2m$. At even order the scalar factors multiply to one; at odd
order they multiply to $(-1)^m$. Substitution of \eqref{cp:eq:ATeval}
gives all four displayed formulas, including the factors two.
Their degrees sum to $2m^2$. Their values are real, so conjugation gives
the identical formulas at $-\ii$.
\end{proof}

\section{The exceptional cancellation is exactly three orders}
\label{cp:sec:triple}

Let $F_r(n)$ be the polynomial obtained from
\eqref{cp:eq:quart0}--\eqref{cp:eq:quart3} by substituting $L=(n-r)/4$.
Thus $F_r(n)=\Ht_{4m+1}(n;\ii)$ on the residue $n\equiv r\pmod4$.
Remove the odd-residue signs by setting
\[
 G_0=F_0,\quad G_2=F_2,\quad
 G_1=(-1)^m F_1,\quad G_3=(-1)^m F_3.
\]
In this section these polynomials are expressed in the centered variable
$x=n+2m+1/2$. All four have degree $\delta=2m^2$ and the same positive
leading coefficient
\begin{equation}
 \Lambda_m=
 \frac{2^{-2m^2}}
 {\displaystyle
  \prod_{1\le u\le v\le m}(u+v-1)
  \prod_{1\le u\le v\le m-1}(u+v)^2
  \prod_{1\le u\le v\le m}(u+v)}.
 \label{cp:eq:Lambda}
\end{equation}
For $G_1$, the ratio to the displayed leading coefficient is
$2\rise m m/\rise{m+1}m=1$. Reflection gives
\begin{equation}
 G_3(x)=G_0(-x),\qquad G_2(x)=G_1(-x).
 \label{cp:eq:Greflection}
\end{equation}
One can verify these identities from the factors. Alternatively,
\eqref{cp:eq:negativegeneral} at $k=4m+1,t=\ii$ reads
$\widetilde\Ht_k(-n-k;\ii)=(-1)^m\widetilde\Ht_k(n;\ii)$ for integer
$n$; it swaps residues $0,3$ and $1,2$. Since each residue agrees with a
polynomial at infinitely many integers, this proves
\eqref{cp:eq:Greflection} as a polynomial identity.

\subsection{The relevant discrete Fourier coefficient}
For any period-four polynomial representation,
$F_r(n)=\sum_{j=0}^3\ii^{jr}A_j(n)$, so
$A_j(n)=\frac14\sum_{r=0}^3\ii^{-jr}F_r(n)$.
The potentially exceptional phase is
$\lambda_{\rm bad}=(-1)^{m-1}$. In either parity of $m$, its grouped
polynomial is
\begin{equation}
 P_{\rm bad}(x)=\frac14(G_0-G_1+G_2-G_3)
 =\frac12\bigl(\operatorname{odd}G_0-\operatorname{odd}G_1\bigr),
 \label{cp:eq:badFourier}
\end{equation}
where $\operatorname{odd}G=(G(x)-G(-x))/2$.
Thus three consecutive candidate odd-degree coefficients can be inspected
without expanding the full high-degree products.

\subsection{Root power sums through degree five}
Write $G_j(x)=\Lambda_m\prod_{u\in\mathcal R_j}(x+u)$ for $j=0,1$.
Let $c=2m+1/2$ and let a triangular set of size $s$ mean
$1\le u\le v\le s$. The two offset multisets are
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal R_0:\quad&
 2(u+v)-2-c\quad(s=m),\\[-2pt]
 &2(u+v)-c\quad(s=m-1),\text{ twice},\\[-2pt]
 &2(u+v)-c\quad(s=m);\\[3pt]
 \mathcal R_1:\quad&
 2(u+v)+1-c\quad(s=m-1),\\[-2pt]
 &2(u+v)-1-c\quad(s=m-1),\\[-2pt]
 &2(u+v)-1-c\quad(s=m),\text{ twice}.
\end{align*}
Put $p_j^{(r)}=\sum_{u\in\mathcal R_r}u^j$,
$\overline p_j=(p_j^{(0)}+p_j^{(1)})/2$, and
$\Delta p_j=p_j^{(0)}-p_j^{(1)}$. Direct summation gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 j&\overline p_j&\Delta p_j\\ \hline
 1&m&0\\
 2& m^2(8m^2+1)/6&2m\\
 3& m(16m^2-9)/4&3m^2\\
 4&\text{not needed}&m(16m^2-11)\\
 5&\text{not needed}&\frac52m^2(8m^2-5).
\end{array}
 \label{cp:eq:powersums}
\end{equation}
These are finite polynomial identities, not asymptotic approximations.
A reproducible way to check every entry is to use
\begin{equation}
 T_s(b,j)=\sum_{v=1}^s\sum_{u=1}^v
       (2(u+v)+b-c)^j
 =\sum_{h=0}^j\binom jh2^h(b-c)^{j-h}
   \sum_{v=1}^s\sum_{r=0}^h\binom hr v^{h-r}\sum_{u=1}^v u^r.
 \label{cp:eq:triangularsum}
\end{equation}
The two power sums are respectively
$T_m(-2,j)+2T_{m-1}(0,j)+T_m(0,j)$ and
$T_{m-1}(1,j)+T_{m-1}(-1,j)+2T_m(-1,j)$.
The usual finite sums of powers through degree six evaluate the right side
of \eqref{cp:eq:triangularsum} and give \eqref{cp:eq:powersums} for every integer
$m\ge1$. The included symbolic verifier repeats this polynomial calculation
with $m$ left as a symbol.

Let $e_j^{(r)}$ be the elementary symmetric functions of the respective
multisets. Newton's identities yield
\begin{equation}
 \Delta e_1=0,\qquad \Delta e_3=0,\qquad
 \boxed{\Delta e_5=-m^2(m^2-1).}
 \label{cp:eq:elementarydifferences}
\end{equation}
For an explicit algebra check, $e_3=(p_1^3-3p_1p_2+2p_3)/6$, while
\[
 120e_5=p_1^5-10p_1^3p_2+15p_1p_2^2+20p_1^2p_3
        -20p_2p_3-30p_1p_4+24p_5.
\]
Subtracting between the two multisets, the terms containing
$\overline p_2$ cancel. What remains is
\[
 120\Delta e_5=40m^4-40m\overline p_3+30m^2
             =-120m^2(m^2-1),
\]
which proves the last identity without relying on an unevaluated computer
output.

\begin{theorem}[Exact exceptional degree]
\label{cp:thm:triple}
For $m\ge2$, the polynomial of phase $(-1)^{m-1}$ in
$\Ht_{4m+1}(n;\ii)$ has degree $2m^2-5$ and leading term
\begin{equation}
 -\frac{\Lambda_m}{2}m^2(m^2-1)x^{2m^2-5},
 \qquad x=n+2m+1/2.
 \label{cp:eq:badleading}
\end{equation}
For $m=1$ that phase polynomial is identically zero.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Since $\delta$ is even, the odd parts of $G_0,G_1$ have their first three
possible degrees $\delta-1,\delta-3,\delta-5$.
Equation~\eqref{cp:eq:elementarydifferences}, inserted into
\eqref{cp:eq:badFourier}, cancels the first two and gives the nonzero third
coefficient \eqref{cp:eq:badleading}. For $m=1$, the polynomials have degree
two, and their odd linear terms agree, so \eqref{cp:eq:badFourier} vanishes.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Completion of Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}]
Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:tie} settles every unique-maximum residue. Reflection
\eqref{cp:eq:paritypair} settles the leading cancellation criterion in the
single possible tied residue. Proposition~\ref{cp:prop:ordinary} gives an
exact one-degree loss except at the stated fourth roots, including the
separately treated constant disappearance case.
In the exceptional family $D=2m^2-2$; Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:triple} gives
exponent $1+(2m^2-5)=D-2$ for $m\ge2$ and zero for $m=1$.
Conjugation treats $-\ii$. Finally Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:minimality} converts
these exact grouped degrees to the reduced denominator and minimal
recurrence. This proves every case of the theorem.
\end{proof}

\section{Consequences and explicit forms}
\label{cp:sec:consequences}

\subsection{Every complex parameter, not just roots of unity}
\begin{corollary}[Complete complex-parameter classification]
\label{cp:cor:complex}
For $k=1$ the reduced denominator is $1-z$ for all $t$. For $k\ge2$:
\begin{equation}
 \QQ_{k,t}(z)=
 \begin{cases}
 1-z,&t=0,\\
 (1-z)^{a(k-a)+1},&t=1,\\
 (1-z)^{ab+1}(1+z)^{(a-1)(b+1)+1},&t=-1,\\
 \displaystyle\prod_{q=0}^{M}(1-t^qz)^{d_q+1},
       &t\ne0\text{ is not a root of unity},\\
 \displaystyle\prod_{\rho=0}^{\ell-1}(1-t^\rho z)^{e_\rho},
       &t\text{ has order }\ell\ge3,
 \end{cases}
 \label{cp:eq:allcomplex}
\end{equation}
where the last exponents are given in Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At $t=0$ the Schur alphabet has exactly $a$ nonzero entries, all one,
and the rectangular tableau is unique. At $t=1$ the one-cluster formula
in Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:cluster} has degree $a(k-a)$.
At $t=-1$ there are two nodes: $1$ of multiplicity $k-1$ and $-1$ of
multiplicity one. The allocations select respectively $a$ or $a-1$
entries from the first cluster. Their degrees are $ab$ and
$(a-1)(b+1)$, and their distinct phases have nonzero leading coefficients.
Away from roots of unity all products $t^q$ are distinct, so
\eqref{cp:eq:spectral} and Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:minimality} apply directly.
The remaining case is the main theorem. The first three cases and the
generic real case agree with the preceding repository report.
\end{proof}

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch 66: the preceding repository report is Part~II;
the cases $t=0,\pm1$ are its Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:real}, and the generic
case is its Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:recurrence-main}, stated there over
$\Q(t)$ and at real $t\notin\{0,\pm1\}$. The line for complex $t$
that is not a root of unity extends that theorem to the complex plane.]

\subsection{Compact formulas at primitive fourth roots}
\begin{corollary}[Odd shifts at $t=\ii$]
\label{cp:cor:fourth}
For $m\ge1$, put $\delta=2m^2$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \QQ_{4m+1,\ii}(z)=
 (1-(-1)^m z)^{\delta+1}
 (1+(-1)^m z)^{\max(0,\delta-4)}
 (1+z^2)^\delta.
 \label{cp:eq:fourth1}
\end{equation}
For $m\ge0$, put $\eta=2m(m+1)$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \QQ_{4m+3,\ii}(z)=
 (1-z^2)^{\eta+1}
 (1-(-1)^m\ii z)^{\eta+1}
 (1+(-1)^m\ii z)^{\max(0,\eta-2)}.
 \label{cp:eq:fourth3}
\end{equation}
At $-\ii$ take complex conjugates.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $k=4m+1$, the index nearest the center in the central residue is
$q=2m$, of degree $\delta$. The two adjacent residues have maximal degree
$\delta-1$, and the opposite real phase has the exceptional exponent
$\max(0,\delta-4)$.
For $k=4m+3$, the two real phases and the central imaginary phase have
maximal degree $\eta$; the opposite imaginary phase has degree
$\eta-3$ after ordinary cancellation. The small case $m=0$ has no such
phase and is covered by the maximum with zero.
\end{proof}

For example, Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:quartic} at $m=1$ gives particularly simple
integer values:
\begin{align*}
 \Ht_5(4L;\ii)&=(2L+1)(L+1),&
 \Ht_5(4L+1;\ii)&=-2(L+1)^2,\\
 \Ht_5(4L+2;\ii)&=2(L+1)^2,&
 \Ht_5(4L+3;\ii)&=-(2L+3)(L+1).
\end{align*}
The phase $+1$ cancels identically, not merely in its highest coefficient.
The denominator is $(1+z)^3(1+z^2)^2$.

\subsection{Quasipolynomial periods and exact reconstruction}
\begin{corollary}[Quasipolynomial structure]
\label{cp:cor:quasipolynomial}
At a root of unity of order $\ell\ge3$, $\Ht_k(n;\zeta)$ is a
quasipolynomial of period dividing $\ell$. Its least quasipolynomial
period is
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{lcm}\{\ord(\zeta^\rho):e_\rho>0\}.
 \label{cp:eq:leastperiod}
\end{equation}
On each residue it is a polynomial of degree at most
$\max_\rho(e_\rho-1)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each surviving mode is a polynomial times $\zeta^{\rho n}$, which is
periodic in $n$. Conversely a quasipolynomial of period $s$ has only
$s$th roots of unity as its exponential phases, by finite Fourier inversion
on its residue polynomials. Independence in Lemma~\ref{cp:lem:minimality}
forces every surviving phase to have order dividing $s$.
\end{proof}

This gives an exact reconstruction procedure, not just an existence result.
Compute the exponents from the main theorem, then either compute the
canonical confluent polynomials and combine their equal phases, or compute
sufficient initial values by Jacobi--Trudi and interpolate the bounded-degree
polynomial on each residue. The proof supplies the a priori bound that makes
such interpolation a certified finite task in exact arithmetic. The
Berlekamp--Massey experiments included with this article serve as independent
checks, rather than as the source of that bound.

For the unnormalized determinants $D_k(n;\zeta)$, the multiplier in
\eqref{cp:eq:interface} is a nonzero periodic quadratic phase, of period
dividing $4\ell$. Thus they too are quasipolynomials, of period dividing
$4\ell$. Their \emph{minimal} recurrences need a further Fourier
combination and are not asserted to equal \eqref{cp:eq:answer}.

\subsection{A quadratic recurrence-order law}
\begin{corollary}[Fixed cyclotomic order]
\label{cp:cor:order}
Let $N_{k,\ell}=\sum_\rho e_\rho$ and fix $\ell\ge3$.
For $M=k-1\ge\ell$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\ell(M^2-\ell^2)}8-3
 \ \le\ N_{k,\ell}\ \le\ \frac{\ell M^2}8+\ell.
 \label{cp:eq:orderbounds}
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{equation}
 N_{k,\ell}=\frac{\ell(k-1)^2}{8}+O_\ell(1)
 \qquad(k\to\infty).
 \label{cp:eq:orderasymptotic}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In each residue choose an index nearest to $M/2$. Its distance is at most
$\ell/2$, and $M\ge\ell$ ensures that a nearest choice lies in $[0,M]$.
Since $q(M-q)/2=M^2/8-(q-M/2)^2/2$, the baseline exponent lies between
$(M^2-\ell^2)/8$ and $M^2/8+1$. Sum over residues and use the fact that
the total correction is at most three.
\end{proof}
Generically the order is
$\sum_{q=0}^{k-1}(1+d_q)=k^3/12+O(k^2)$. Therefore a fixed
cyclotomic order changes the growth from cubic in the shift to quadratic.
This is a statement about recurrence order, not a bit-complexity assertion:
large exact coefficients and initial-value computation still have costs.

\section{Verification, dependency audit, and formalization prospects}
\label{cp:sec:verification}

\subsection{What the included computations check}
The archive contains an executable Python verifier, exact output records,
and a source audit. The program uses integer arithmetic, rational arithmetic,
Gaussian-rational arithmetic, symbolic polynomial arithmetic, and finite
fields; it never estimates pole orders from floating-point roots.

The independent checks comprise the following finite tasks.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Canonical confluent polynomials for $2\le k\le7$ at $t=2,3$,
constructed by Laplace expansion rather than by the claimed coefficient
formulas. All 54 modes are checked for degree, leading coefficient,
centered first coefficient, and reflection. An additional 22 modes with
other cluster multiplicities check the general coefficient formula.
\item The four product formulas for $1\le m\le4$, $0\le n<20$,
checked against Jacobi--Trudi over $\Q(\ii)$: 80 exact values.
\item The power-sum and Newton identities in
\eqref{cp:eq:powersums}--\eqref{cp:eq:elementarydifferences}, checked with $m$
left symbolic. This is polynomial identity verification, not a list of
numerical samples.
\item Finite-field recurrence reconstruction for $2\le k\le17$ and
$3\le\ell\le17$: 240 cases. For each order a prime $p>10^6$ with
$p\equiv1\pmod\ell$ is used, and a primitive $\ell$th root is formed
in $\mathbb F_p$. At least twice the baseline denominator degree plus
eight sequence terms are computed. Berlekamp--Massey output is factored
into the predicted linear factors and compared with the exponent list.
\item A separate finite check compares the original normalized Hankel
formula with Schur evaluation at rational and Gaussian-rational parameters.
This includes $1\le k\le8$, $0\le n\le5$, and $t=2,-2,\ii$: 144 comparisons.
The generated JSON report records these ranges and counts.
\end{enumerate}
The finite-field test is a check of specialized finite sequences, not an
independent characteristic-zero proof and not a theorem for untested
parameters. Bad reduction could in principle conceal or create a
cancellation; the all-index arguments above, not the modular experiments,
establish the characteristic-zero classification. No claim is made that
this script certifies the repository as a whole.

\path{data/03-cyclotomic-poles-verification.json} (with the console log
\path{data/03-cyclotomic-poles-verification.log}) and
\path{data/03-cyclotomic-poles-modular_checks.json}. The file
\path{data/verification.json} is Part~I's record
(Section~\ref{sec:verify}). The verifier has no output option: it always
writes \path{verification.json} and \path{modular_checks.json} into the
\path{data/} directory beside its own \path{code/} directory, and it
imports its helper under the delivered name \texttt{modular}. It must
therefore be run only on a copy with the delivered layout
(Appendix~\ref{cp:app:package}). Run that way on 30 September 2026
(Python 3.13.5, SymPy 1.14.0), it passed every check; its
\path{modular_checks.json} equals the record after CRLF$\to$LF (the
script writes CRLF on Windows), and its \path{verification.json} differs
only in the fields \texttt{utc} and \texttt{elapsed\_seconds}. During
the intake the pole exponents were also recomputed from scratch for
$3\le\ell\le8$, $1\le k\le15$ (Jacobi--Trudi in exact
$\Z[x]/\Phi_\ell(x)$, lowering each phase exponent while the operator
still annihilates): all 90 cases agree with Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}.]

\subsection{Logical dependencies}
The new spectral argument depends on a short, explicit list of ingredients.
The bialternant identity and Vandermonde confluence give the canonical
polynomials. Reflection and the elementary concavity lemma restrict their
collisions. The first-coefficient theorem resolves every ordinary collision.
The fourth-root Gram reduction, beta determinant calculation, and Newton
identities resolve the only exception. Finally exponential-polynomial
independence proves minimality, not just annihilation.

The spectral theorem can be formalized without first constructing the whole
Hankel-moment development: take $s_{(n^a)}(X_k(t))$ as the initial definition.
To transfer it to the original moments one needs exactly
\eqref{cp:eq:interface}. The fourth-root proof as written uses the same
identification together with the directly verified odd-shift block
elimination in Section~\ref{cp:sec:quartic}. All assumptions are thus explicit;
no numerical recurrence is promoted to an all-index theorem.

\subsection{A practical proof-assistant route}
A realistic first stage is entirely algebraic: formalize exponential
polynomials, their annihilators, and exact minimality for distinct nonzero
phases. Next prove the quadratic maximum lemma and implement the integer
exponent function from Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}. These steps do not require
analysis or infinite series.

The main algebraic effort is then the canonical confluent determinant and
its first two coefficients. The proof in Section~\ref{cp:sec:jet} can be
translated into finite polynomial identities by clearing all differences
$\alpha_i-\alpha_j$. Formal power series in $\epsilon$ are one route;
a finite Taylor-jet ring truncated after degree $d+1$ avoids an analytic
limit. The divisibility of alternating polynomials by Vandermondes should
be kept as a reusable lemma.

For the exceptional case, formalize the beta determinant in the factorial
specializations actually used, rather than importing real integration.
The triangular power sums and Newton identities are elementary polynomial
identities and are suitable for ring normalization with generated,
independently checkable coefficient tables. Only after those components are
in place is it useful to attach the original Hankel--Schur interface.
No Lean or Rocq implementation of these new results is included or claimed.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{cp:sec:further}
The classification suggests several concrete follow-up problems. The
questions below are not used in the proofs.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch 66: none of these questions repeats one of
Part~II's. Section~\ref{cp:q:middle} and Section~\ref{cp:q:certified}
continue Part~II's topic ``Broader noncollision criteria and certified
algorithms'' (Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-noncollision}), on which
Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:jet} bears; Section~\ref{cp:q:numerators} is related
to Part~II's Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-conj17} on Cigler's Conjecture~17, as
it says; Section~\ref{cp:q:detuning} moves the parameter near a root of
unity, whereas Part~II's ``uniform crossover as the poles merge'' moves it
to $t=1$.]

\subsection{More than one middle variable}
\label{cp:q:middle}
Replace the alphabet $(1^{[a]},t,(t^2)^{[b]})$ by
$(1^{[a]},t^{[c]},(t^2)^{[b]})$ with $c\ge2$.
Several allocations can then have the same product even before a
root-of-unity specialization. Determine their combined degrees and the
exact minimal recurrence. The first-coefficient formula remains available,
but the unique-allocation mechanism \eqref{cp:eq:allocation} disappears.
A useful first target is $c=2$, where one can enumerate the competing
allocations explicitly.

\subsection{A general bound on cancellation depth}
\label{cp:q:depth}
For arbitrary repeated-node rectangular Schur polynomials, can the maximal
degree lost when products collide be bounded in terms of the number of
clusters, rather than the total multiplicity? The present family has loss
at most three, and its exceptional loss is detected by a fifth symmetric
coefficient. A structural theorem would explain when a finite number of
Taylor coefficients suffices to establish minimality.

\subsection{Higher coefficients of a canonical spectral mode}
\label{cp:q:higher}
Extend Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:jet} to the second and third centered coefficients
in closed cluster notation. Expanding the finite functional proof further
introduces quadratic and cubic characteristic polynomials in the selected
sums. Such formulas could replace special-purpose product evaluations and
make multi-cluster collision tests systematic.

\subsection{Why do fourth roots force the additional cancellation?}
\label{cp:q:fourth}
The proof here is an explicit algebraic one: the first and third elementary
symmetric differences vanish and the fifth does not. Is there a character-
theoretic or geometric explanation that predicts this pattern before
calculation? In particular, does a symmetry of the relevant rectangular
Schur module identify the missing low-order terms in the bad phase?
Any such explanation should recover the coefficient
$-\Lambda_m m^2(m^2-1)/2$, not merely predict vanishing.

\subsection{Detuning a root of unity}
\label{cp:q:detuning}
Investigate the transition when $t=\zeta e^{u/n}$ with $u$ in a bounded
complex set. Distinct generic phases then remain separated on a scale
visible to the polynomial prefactors, while at $u=0$ they cancel.
Uniform asymptotic formulas should distinguish the ordinary one-order
transition from the exceptional three-order one. This is a separate
moving-parameter problem; fixed-$t$ recurrence minimality alone does not
solve it.

\subsection{Numerators and zeros at cyclotomic parameters}
\label{cp:q:numerators}
The denominator is now known exactly. Determine the reduced numerator,
its reciprocity, and its zero set at roots of unity. Which of its
coefficients admit an enumerative or signed representation? This problem
is related to, but should not be conflated with, the full coefficient
assertions in Cigler's Conjecture~17 and the corrected generic numerator
claims in the repository.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch 70: the generic coefficient
assertions of Conjecture~17 are proved, with a corrected sign, in Part~IV
(Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}). That does not answer this question, which
concerns the reduced numerator at roots of unity.]

\subsection{Original, unnormalized Hankel determinants}
\label{cp:q:unnormalized}
Classify the minimal recurrence of $D_k(n;\zeta)$ itself, including the
periodic quadratic phase in \eqref{cp:eq:interface}. Its Fourier transform
can mix the surviving $\Ht$ phases, so the answer need not be a simple
relabeling of the denominator obtained here. Exact Gauss-sum identities
may provide a useful description of this additional mixing.

\subsection{Joint growth of the shift and cyclotomic order}
\label{cp:q:joint}
Corollary~\ref{cp:cor:order} fixes $\ell$. Determine a uniform recurrence-order
asymptotic when $\ell/k$ approaches a nonzero limit, and describe the
transition between the quadratic and cubic regimes. The finite maximum
formula reduces this to a lattice-point problem with a small cancellation
correction, making this an accessible next theorem rather than an
unstructured asymptotic conjecture.

\subsection{Certified recurrence generation}
\label{cp:q:certified}
Produce a proof-producing implementation that accepts $k$ and a cyclotomic
order and returns the exact denominator, its minimality certificate, and
a certified evaluator. A first practical deliverable is the exponent
function plus a checked proof of the concavity and reflection reductions;
the later confluence and quartic certificates can be attached modularly.

\section{Conclusion}
The distinction between an annihilating denominator and a reduced denominator
is decisive at roots of unity. For Cigler's normalized Hankel polynomials,
strict concavity reduces all possible leading cancellations to one residue.
Reflection gives an exact parity test, a general centered coefficient formula
settles every ordinary case, and an explicit fourth-root product calculation
proves the unique three-order exception. The resulting theorem classifies
the minimal recurrence at every complex parameter and closes the precise
cyclotomic gap identified in the inspected ProveIt report.

The further questions concern genuinely additional work: larger middle
multiplicity, deeper multi-cluster collisions, moving-parameter transitions,
numerators, and proof-assistant integration. They are not needed to complete
the classification proved here.

\renewcommand{\thesection}{\Alph{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{cp.app.\Alph{section}}
\setcounter{section}{4}
\section{An elementary beta determinant and the product evaluations}
\label{cp:app:beta}
This appendix supplies the normalizations used in
\eqref{cp:eq:ATeval} and \eqref{cp:eq:endpoints}. Although these evaluations
belong to classical determinant and orthogonal-polynomial theory
\cite{Kratt}, the required determinant can be proved directly.

\begin{lemma}[Beta-moment determinant]
\label{cp:lem:betadet}
For positive real $A,B$ and $L\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \det\bigl(\mathrm B(A+i+j,B)\bigr)_{0\le i,j<L}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{L-1}
   \frac{j!\,\Gamma(A+j)\Gamma(B+j)}{\Gamma(A+B+L+j-1)}.
 \label{cp:eq:betadet}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set $X_i=A+i$. Factor $\mathrm B(X_i,B)$ from row $i$.
The remaining entry in column $j$ is
$\rise{X_i}j/\rise{X_i+B}j$.
For arbitrary distinct $X_i$ one has
\begin{equation}
 \det\left(\frac{\rise{X_i}j}{\rise{X_i+B}j}\right)_{i,j<L}
 =\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{L-1}\rise Bj
              \prod_{i<r}(X_r-X_i)}
        {\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{L-1}\rise{X_i+B}{L-1}}.
 \label{cp:eq:rationaldet}
\end{equation}
To prove this, multiply row $i$ by its denominator on the right.
Column $j$ becomes the polynomial
$f_j(X)=\rise Xj\rise{X+B+j}{L-1-j}$ of degree $L-1$.
Its evaluation determinant is its coefficient determinant times the
Vandermonde. Evaluate at $X_i=-i$: the matrix is lower triangular,
with diagonal $(-1)^i i!\rise B{L-1-i}$.
The Vandermonde at these points is
$(-1)^{\binom L2}\prod_i i!$. Their quotient is
$\prod_j\rise Bj$, proving \eqref{cp:eq:rationaldet}.
Substitute $X_i=A+i$, use
$\prod_{i<r}(r-i)=\prod_j j!$, and simplify the gamma factors.
The empty determinant is immediate.
\end{proof}

The binomial and Catalan moments have beta representations
\begin{equation}
 \binom{2j}{j}=\frac{4^j}{\pi}\mathrm B(j+1/2,1/2),\qquad
 C_j=\frac{2\cdot4^j}{\pi}\mathrm B(j+1/2,3/2).
 \label{cp:eq:betamoments}
\end{equation}
They follow from the gamma recurrence and the half-integer formula.
Applying \eqref{cp:eq:betadet} after factoring the powers of four gives
\begin{align}
 A_s(L)&=4^{Ls+L(L-1)}\pi^{-L}
  \prod_{j=0}^{L-1}
   \frac{j!\Gamma(s+j+1/2)\Gamma(j+1/2)}{\Gamma(s+L+j)},
 \label{cp:eq:Aproduct}\\
 T_s(L)&=4^{Ls+L(L-1)}(2/\pi)^L
  \prod_{j=0}^{L-1}
   \frac{j!\Gamma(s+j+1/2)\Gamma(j+3/2)}{\Gamma(s+L+j+1)}.
 \label{cp:eq:Tproduct}
\end{align}
Their quotient immediately yields the third formula in
\eqref{cp:eq:ATeval}.
For an elementary check of the first two, the factorial products give
$A_1(L)=2^L$, $T_1(L)=1$, and
\begin{align*}
 \frac{A_{s+1}(L)}{A_s(L)}
 &=4^L\frac{\rise{s+1/2}L}{\rise{s+L}L}
   =\frac{\rise{2L+s}s}{\rise ss},\\
 \frac{T_{s+1}(L)}{T_s(L)}
 &=4^L\frac{\rise{s+1/2}L}{\rise{s+L+1}L}
   =\frac{\rise{2L+s+1}s}{\rise{s+1}s}.
\end{align*}
The right sides are exactly the successive triangular factors in
\eqref{cp:eq:alpha} and \eqref{cp:eq:beta}. Induction on $s$ proves
\eqref{cp:eq:ATeval} for all $s\ge1$, $L\ge0$.

Finally, the Christoffel determinant identity follows by appending the row
$1,y,\ldots,y^L$ to the moment matrix: the resulting determinant divided
by the size-$L$ moment determinant is the monic orthogonal polynomial.
Putting $y=1$ and subtracting adjacent columns identifies its value with
the determinant ratio for weights $(1-y)w$ and $w$.
For beta weight, \eqref{cp:eq:betadet} gives
\begin{equation}
 \pi_L(1)=\frac{D_L(A,B+1)}{D_L(A,B)}
         =\frac{\rise BL}{\rise{A+B+L-1}L}.
 \label{cp:eq:betaendpoint}
\end{equation}
Scaling $y=x^2/4$ proves \eqref{cp:eq:endpoints}. Positivity of the beta
weights ensures all the moment determinants used to define these monic
polynomials are nonzero. The final identities themselves are polynomial
or rational identities and need no limiting interpretation.

\section{A minimal exponent algorithm}
\label{cp:app:algorithm}
The following pseudocode is an exact integer algorithm for
Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}. It does not need a numerical approximation to a
root of unity.
\begin{verbatim}
cyclotomic_exponents(k, ell):
    require k >= 1 and ell >= 3
    M = k - 1
    a = floor(k/2)
    e = array of ell zeros
    for q in 0..M:
        e[q mod ell] = max(e[q mod ell], 1 + floor(q*(M-q)/2))
    if ell > M or (ell-M) is odd:
        return e
    q = (M-ell)/2
    D = floor((M*M-ell*ell)/8)
    cancels = (a+D is odd) if k is even else (D is even)
    if cancels:
        e[q mod ell] = max(0, D-2) if ell == 4 and k mod 4 == 1 else D
    return e
\end{verbatim}
A direct implementation uses $O(k+\ell)$ elementary integer operations
and $O(\ell)$ storage for a dense output array. These are arithmetic-operation
counts; bit costs depend on the sizes of the integer indices. A sparse map
avoids allocating $\ell$ entries when $\ell$ is large compared with $k$.
The coefficient field for the actual recurrence is $\Q(\zeta)$, and
its factorized form is usually preferable to expanding it.

\section{Package and reproduction}
\label{cp:app:package}
The package has a standalone \texttt{article.tex}, its compiled
\texttt{article.pdf}, a \texttt{README.md}, a claim-status file, a source
audit, and code and data directories. Compile the paper with
\begin{verbatim}
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{verbatim}
Run the exact verification suite from the package root with
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py
\end{verbatim}
It writes \texttt{data/verification.json} and
\texttt{data/modular\_checks.json}. The delivered record reports the versions
and counts actually used. Successful finite checks are explicitly separated
from the all-index mathematical proofs. No network access, proprietary
computer algebra system, or repository checkout is required to rerun them.

\phantomsection\part*{Part IV.\quad Polynomial spectral blocks and Cigler's Conjecture 17}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part IV.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } Polynomial spectral blocks and Cigler's Conjecture 17}

\noindent\emph{Part~IV was added on 30 September 2026 in batch~70 of
ProveIt's incoming-report intake, from one manuscript written against the
pinned state of Parts~I and~II. It answers Part~II's research topic ``Full
coefficient structure in Cigler's Conjecture~17''
(Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-conj17}) in a sign-corrected form. It proves that,
after Cigler's denominator is cleared, the normalized determinants split
into blocks that are polynomials in $t$ and integer-valued in $n$, with the
triangular exponents and $t$-degrees that Conjecture~17 proposes, exact
$n$-degrees and explicit leading terms. It also proves that the reciprocity
sign printed in Cigler's equation~(83) is false for the shifts
$K\equiv1\pmod4$, and proves the correct sign. Its Sections~35--44 and
Appendices~H--I are the manuscript's Sections~1--10 and Appendices~A--B.
Parts~I--III are unchanged except for dated pointers to this addition,
marked \textup{[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: \dots]}.}

\section{The problem and the result}
\label{sb:sec:introduction}

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: Cigler's moment
family and its normalization (Section~\ref{sb:sec:input} and the subsection
before it); the main theorem, Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}, on polynomial
spectral blocks $\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)\in\IntZ[t]$ with exact bidegrees and
corrected reciprocity; the confluent bialternant with its Laplace grouping
by the unique allocation of each spectral exponent
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:bialternant}); two divisibility mechanisms, an exact
triangular valuation at $t=0$ (Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:t-zero}) and a
jet-collision valuation with a two-server counting lemma at $t=1$
(Lemmas~\ref{sb:lem:jet-collision}--\ref{sb:lem:t-one}), giving
polynomiality and integer values (Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:polynomiality});
closed leading coefficients in the width~$n$ for even and odd sectors
\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even}, \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd} and the exact
$n$-degree (Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:n-degree}); Schur reciprocity, the exact
$t$-degree (Proposition~\ref{sb:prop:t-degree}), the failure of the printed
sign (Section~\ref{sb:sec:printed-sign}) and the boundary blocks
(Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:boundary}); worked examples for $K\le5$ and a table
for $K\le8$ (Section~\ref{sb:sec:examples}); a finite algorithm
(Algorithm~\ref{sb:alg:blocks}), its verifier and complexity remarks
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:verification}); a four-layer formalization route
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:formalization}); nine research questions
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:further}); a conclusion; the exponent algebra behind
the leading terms (Appendix~\ref{sb:app:leading-algebra}); and package notes
(Appendix~\ref{sb:app:package}). Every result, proof, example, remark,
limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.

\paragraph{What Part~IV proves, and what it corrects.}
Cigler's Conjecture~17 \cite{Cigler} (printed pp.~22--23) proposes the
expansion \eqref{sb:eq:main-expansion} with polynomial blocks of
$t$-degree $q(K-1-q)$ and a reciprocity between the blocks $q$ and
$K-1-q$ whose sign his equation~(83) prints as $(-1)^{\lfloor K/2\rfloor-1}$
for every shift. Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main} proves the expansion, the
$t$-degrees and the polynomiality, and adds the integrality in~$n$, the
$n$-degrees and the leading terms. Its reciprocity sign $\epsK_K$
\eqref{sb:eq:epsilon}, however, is not the printed one: the two agree for
every even $K$ and every $K\equiv3\pmod4$, and for every
$K\equiv1\pmod4$ the printed sign is $-1$ where the true sign is $+1$.
Section~\ref{sb:sec:printed-sign} shows from the central block $\Cblock_{5,2}$
that Cigler himself displays (his printed page~24) that the printed
relation already fails at $K=5$, independently of any proof here. \emph{The
literal printed Conjecture~17 is therefore false, and Part~IV proves a
sign-corrected form of it, not the conjecture as printed.} Wherever the
manuscript says that it proves or resolves Conjecture~17 (its title, the name
of Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}, its abstract and its first status box), read
``proves Conjecture~17 with the sign of~(83) corrected''; its status box
says as much itself. This is the same situation as in Part~II, which proved
a corrected Conjecture~18 after finding the positive sign of Cigler's
equation~(85) false (Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:numerator} and
\eqref{rs:eq:G4}), but the residue classes differ: Cigler's~(85) fails for
shifts $k=2a$ with $a$ even, that is for $4\mid k$, whereas his~(83) fails
for $K\equiv1\pmod4$. The manuscript states the failure range as
``$K\equiv1\pmod4$ and $K\ge5$''; at $K=1$ the printed sign also fails,
trivially (Section~\ref{sb:sec:printed-sign} carries a note). The
correction concerns Cigler's paper, not this repository, which never
asserted his equation~(83) and called Conjecture~17 open.

\paragraph{Relation to other reports.} The sibling reports
\path{cigler-conjecture-8-schur} (Cigler's Conjecture~8, for the different
family $b_n(t)$) and \path{ballot-polynomial-hankel-determinants}
(Conjectures~13--15, the family $a_n(t)$) do not treat Conjecture~17, and
Part~IV does not treat their families. The manuscript expects its
jet-collision mechanism to apply beyond this Hankel family (its fourth
contribution, and the question in Section~\ref{sb:q:clusters}); only the
three-cluster alphabet of this report is proved here.

\paragraph{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Duplicated material.} The manuscript credits its inputs to
the report, and they are printed in both parts.
\eqref{sb:eq:moments}, \eqref{sb:eq:hankel} and
\eqref{sb:eq:normalized-hankel} are Part~II's \eqref{rs:eq:moments},
\eqref{rs:eq:D} and \eqref{rs:eq:H} (with $\mu_m=c_m$ and $\Delta_K=D_k$);
the Schur identity \eqref{sb:eq:schur-identity} is Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:schur}; the spectral decomposition
\eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral} with its $n$-degree is
\eqref{rs:eq:spectralH} with \eqref{rs:eq:dq}; the unique allocation
\eqref{sb:eq:unique-allocation} and the degrees \eqref{sb:eq:even-d},
\eqref{sb:eq:odd-d} are \eqref{rs:eq:qallocation} and the two expressions
after \eqref{rs:eq:dq}; the confluent matrix and Vandermonde
\eqref{sb:eq:confluent-matrix}--\eqref{sb:eq:confluent-vandermonde}
specialize \eqref{rs:eq:confluent-entries} and \eqref{rs:eq:confluent-den};
$\beta(m,r)$ \eqref{sb:eq:beta} is Part~II's $B(m,r)$ \eqref{rs:eq:Bfactor},
and the leading coefficient \eqref{sb:eq:cluster-leading} is
\eqref{rs:eq:LC} of Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:cluster}, symbol for symbol. The
first assertion of Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:n-degree} is inherited from
\eqref{rs:eq:spectralH}, as its proof says; it is not a second route. The
reciprocity \eqref{sb:eq:H-reciprocity} is Part~I's \eqref{eq:pal}
(Theorem~\ref{thm:main}), which Part~I proves from its parity
factorization; the manuscript's short Schur argument is a second route.
New in Part~IV are the polynomiality and integrality of the cleared blocks,
their $t$-degrees, the closed leading terms \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even},
\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd} (which refine \eqref{rs:eq:LC} to the blocks
$\Cblock_{K,q}$), the corrected reciprocity of the blocks and the sign
correction. As a consistency check of this merge,
Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:boundary} with \eqref{sb:eq:C-definition} gives
$\Pblock_{K,0}=1/\Den_K(t)$, which is
Part~I's limit function $\F_k(t)$ of \eqref{eq:F}, and the sign $\epsK_K$
coincides, as a function of the shift, with the numerator-reciprocity sign
of Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:numerator} ($(-1)^{a+1}$ for $k=2a$, $+1$ for
odd~$k$). Neither part derives one sign from the other, and no implication
between the two statements is claimed.
\item \emph{Numbering.} The manuscript's Section~$n$ is Section~$n+34$
here, and its Theorem, Lemma, Proposition, Corollary and Algorithm~$n.m$
are $(n+34).m$; its Appendices~A--B are Appendices~H--I. The manuscript
numbers its equations consecutively, (1)--(60), through the whole article;
here they are numbered within sections, as in the rest of the report (the
report README gives the correspondence). This subsection and the next two
precede the manuscript's Section~1.1, which is Section~35.4 here. Its table
of contents is merged into the report's.
\item \emph{Names.} Every label of Part~IV carries the prefix

would otherwise collide with Part~I's. The manuscript's macro

(Section~\ref{sb:sec:further}), and on the delivered commands
(Appendix~\ref{sb:app:package}). All are marked
\textup{[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: \dots]}. The manuscript's
subsections 1.2 and~5.3 and its research questions received labels
\texttt{sb:sec:input}, \texttt{sb:sec:printed-sign} and \texttt{sb:q:\dots}.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The manuscript's title, abstract and status}
\label{sb:sec:front}
The manuscript's title block reads \emph{Polynomial Spectral Blocks for
Cigler's Hankel Determinants}, with the subtitle \emph{A proof of
Conjecture~17, a reciprocity-sign correction, exact bidegrees, and explicit
leading terms}, the author line ``Research report prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov's \texttt{ProveIt} project'' and the date 30~September 2026. As
explained in Section~\ref{sb:sec:merge-provenance}, ``a proof of
Conjecture~17'' means a proof of its sign-corrected form. Its abstract:
\begin{quote}\small
Let $\mu_m(t)$ be Cigler's third polynomial extension of the middle binomial
coefficients, let $\Delta_K(n;t)=\det(\mu_{K+i+j}(t))_{0\le i,j<n}$, and put
\[
 \Ht_K(n;t)=(-1)^{K\binom n2}t^{-\binom n2}\Delta_K(n;t).
\]
A recent report in the \texttt{ProveIt} repository proves the rectangular
Schur identity
\[
 \Ht_K(n;t)=s_{(n^a)}(1^a,t,(t^2)^b),
 \qquad
 a=\left\lfloor\frac K2\right\rfloor,
 \quad b=\left\lfloor\frac{K-1}{2}\right\rfloor,
\]
and obtains the unique exponential--polynomial decomposition
\[
 \Ht_K(n;t)=\sum_{q=0}^{K-1}\Pblock_{K,q}(n;t)t^{qn}.
\]
Its polynomial blocks have exact degrees
\[
 \deg_n\Pblock_{K,q}
 =\left\lfloor\frac{q(K-1-q)}2\right\rfloor.
\]
The same report explicitly leaves unresolved the full coefficient structure
proposed in Cigler's Conjecture~17.

This article closes that gap.  Define
\[
 \Den_K(t)=(1-t)^a(1-t^2)^{ab},\qquad
 \Btri_q=\binom{q+1}{2}.
\]
We prove that there are unique polynomials
$\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)\in\IntZ[t]$ such that
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Ht_K(n;t)=\frac{1}{\Den_K(t)}
 \sum_{q=0}^{K-1}(-1)^q
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)t^{qn+\Btri_q}.}
\]
They have the exact bidegrees conjectured in $t$ and newly determined in $n$:
\[
 \deg_t\Cblock_{K,q}=q(K-1-q),\qquad
 \deg_n\Cblock_{K,q}=\left\lfloor\frac{q(K-1-q)}2\right\rfloor.
\]
The proof starts from the confluent bialternant and groups its Laplace terms by
the unique allocation corresponding to $q$.  Two divisibility mechanisms are
then separated.  A weighted assignment argument at $t=0$ supplies exactly the
triangular power $t^{qn+\binom{q+1}{2}}$.  At $t=1$, a new two-minor jet-collision
lemma shows that every grouped numerator vanishes to order at least $b$; this
cancels the one factor absent from Cigler's denominator.  The argument is
finite and works coefficientwise in the width $n$.

We also determine the leading coefficient in $n$ in closed form.  If
$\beta(m,r)=\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}j!/\prod_{j=m-r}^{m-1}j!$, then for $q=2h$ it is
\[
 \beta(a,a-h)\beta(b,h)(1-t^2)^{\dq_{K,2h}},
\]
and for $q=2h+1$ it is
\[
 (-1)^h\beta(a,a-h-1)\beta(b,h)\chi_K(t)
 (1-t^2)^{\dq_{K,2h+1}},
 \qquad
 \chi_K(t)=\begin{cases}1,&K\text{ even},\\1+t,&K\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Finally, Schur reciprocity gives the corrected relation
\[
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Cblock_{K,K-1-q}(n,t)=\epsK_K\,
 t^{q(K-1-q)}\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t^{-1}),}
\]
where $\epsK_K=-1$ precisely when $4\mid K$, and $\epsK_K=1$ otherwise.
Cigler's printed sign agrees for every even $K$ and for $K\equiv3\pmod4$,
but fails for odd $K\equiv1\pmod4$; its own displayed polynomial
$\Cblock_{5,2}$ is already a counterexample.  An exact symbolic verifier
reconstructs the original Hankel determinants, all blocks, bidegrees,
reciprocities, and leading coefficients through $K=8$.
\end{quote}
Its two status boxes:

\begin{statusbox}{Research status and trust boundary}
The all-index statements below are mathematical proof claims, with complete
arguments supplied in the article.  The work builds on the rectangular-Schur
and confluent spectral theorems already proved in the inspected
\texttt{ProveIt} report at repository snapshot \texttt{\pinIV}.  The repository
itself says that not every assertion of Cigler's Conjecture~17 had been
settled.  The present package has not been independently refereed and has not
been checked in Lean, Rocq, or another proof assistant.  The accompanying
program performs exact finite verification; it is evidence and regression
protection, not a substitute for the proofs.  ``Resolves'' refers to the
mathematical statement of Conjecture~17, with the reciprocity sign corrected as
forced by both the proof and Cigler's own $K=5$ example.  No exhaustive claim
of worldwide priority is made.
\end{statusbox}

\begin{statusbox}{Main contributions}
\begin{enumerate}
\item A proof that every Conjecture~17 spectral numerator is a polynomial in
      $t$, with coefficients integer-valued in $n$.
\item Exact degrees in both variables and an explicit nonzero leading
      coefficient in the width variable $n$.
\item A corrected reciprocity sign: negative exactly for shifts divisible by
      four, positive for every odd shift.
\item A jet-collision divisibility lemma for products of complementary
      confluent minors, applicable beyond this Hankel family.
\item A reproducible exact verifier bridging the original Hankel determinant,
      the rectangular Schur polynomial, and the spectral-block formula.
\end{enumerate}
\end{statusbox}

\noindent The fourth item's ``applicable beyond this Hankel family'' is the
manuscript's expectation: Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:jet-collision} is stated for
arbitrary analytic rows, but the counting Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:two-server} and
the cancellation Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:t-one} concern this alphabet only, and
the general capacity theorem is a question
(Section~\ref{sb:q:clusters}). The abstract's last sentence says that the
verifier ``reconstructs the original Hankel determinants \dots\ through
$K=8$''; its record compares the original Hankel determinants with the
Schur side for $K\le6$, $n\le5$, and checks the blocks against the Schur
side for $K\le8$ (Section~\ref{sb:sec:verification}). The repository statement
quoted in the first box (``not every assertion \dots\ had been settled'') is
Parts~I and~II at the pin.

\subsection{Notation shared with Parts~I--III}
\label{sb:sec:notation}
Part~IV keeps the manuscript's notation. It writes $K$ for the shift that
Parts~I--III call $k$; its $a=\lfloor K/2\rfloor$, $b=\lfloor(K-1)/2\rfloor$
are theirs, and its $K=a+b+1$ is also Part~II's number of variables $K$ in
Section~\ref{rs:sec:cluster} for Cigler's alphabet $\XX_k=(1^a,t,(t^2)^b)$.
Its moments $\mu_m(t)$ are Part~II's $c_m(t)$, its Hankel determinant
$\Delta_K(n;t)$ is $D_k(n;t)$, and $\Ht_K(n;t)$ is $\Ht_k(n;t)$. Its
sans-serif $\Pblock_{K,q}(n;t)$ is Part~II's $P_{k,q}(n;t)$
\eqref{rs:eq:spectralH}, its $\dq_{K,q}$ is Part~II's and Part~III's $d_q$
(so $e_{k,q}=1+\dq_{K,q}$), and its $\beta(m,r)$ is Part~II's $B(m,r)$.
It agrees with Part~II on $s_{(n^A)}$, $A$, $C=K-A$, the nodes $\xi_i$ with
multiplicities $m_i$, the counts $r_i$, $s_i=m_i-r_i$, $L_{\boldsymbol r}$
and $q$. Its $\Den_K(t)=(1-t)^a(1-t^2)^{ab}$ is $1/\F_k(t)$ \eqref{eq:F}.
