\subsection{Cigler's moment family}
For $m\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}
 \mu_m(t)=
 \sum_{j=1}^{m}
 \binom{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}{\lfloor(j-1)/2\rfloor}
 \binom{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}{\lfloor j/2\rfloor}t^{j-1},
 \qquad \mu_0(t)=1.
 \label{sb:eq:moments}
\end{equation}
For a shift $K\ge1$ and an order $n\ge0$, let
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_K(n;t)=\det\bigl(\mu_{K+i+j}(t)\bigr)_{0\le i,j<n},
 \qquad \Delta_K(0;t)=1.
 \label{sb:eq:hankel}
\end{equation}
The normalization relevant to Cigler's Conjecture~17 is
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_K(n;t)=(-1)^{K\binom n2}t^{-\binom n2}\Delta_K(n;t),
 \label{sb:eq:normalized-hankel}
\end{equation}
where the right side is interpreted by polynomial continuation at $t=0$.
The factor $t^{\binom n2}$ divides the determinant, so no Laurent ambiguity
remains.

Throughout the article we use the actual shift $K$ as the principal index and
write
\begin{equation}
 a=\left\lfloor\frac K2\right\rfloor,
 \qquad
 b=\left\lfloor\frac{K-1}{2}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad K=a+b+1.
 \label{sb:eq:ab}
\end{equation}
Thus $a=b$ for odd $K$, whereas $a=b+1$ for even $K$.  Set
\begin{equation}
 \Den_K(t)=(1-t)^a(1-t^2)^{ab},
 \qquad
 \Btri_q=\binom{q+1}{2},
 \label{sb:eq:D-and-B}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \rdeg_{K,q}=q(K-1-q),
 \qquad
 \dq_{K,q}=\left\lfloor\frac{\rdeg_{K,q}}2\right\rfloor.
 \label{sb:eq:r-and-d}
\end{equation}

\subsection{The repository input and the remaining gap}
\label{sb:sec:input}
The inspected \texttt{ProveIt} report proves the identity
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\Ht_K(n;t)=s_{(n^a)}(1^a,t,(t^2)^b).}
 \label{sb:eq:schur-identity}
\end{equation}
It also proves that, over $\Q(t)$, there are unique polynomials in $n$,
$\Pblock_{K,q}(n;t)$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_K(n;t)=\sum_{q=0}^{K-1}\Pblock_{K,q}(n;t)t^{qn},
 \qquad
 \deg_n\Pblock_{K,q}=\dq_{K,q}.
 \label{sb:eq:existing-spectral}
\end{equation}
The uniqueness follows from the linear independence of the sequences
$n^j(t^q)^n$ over $\Q(t)$ when the roots $t^q$ are distinct.  The repository
obtains the exact degree and a nonzero leading coefficient from a general
confluent spectral theorem for rectangular Schur polynomials.

Cigler's Conjecture~17 asks for substantially more than
\eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral}: after extracting a triangular power of $t$, its
coefficient functions should be honest polynomials in $t$, with a precise
degree and a reciprocal pairing.  The current repository report explicitly
lists the full coefficient structure as unresolved.  The following theorem
settles that problem and corrects its sign.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: ``the inspected
\texttt{ProveIt} report'' is Parts~I and~II of this report at the pin
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:merge-provenance}). The identity
\eqref{sb:eq:schur-identity} is Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:schur}; the
decomposition \eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral}, its uniqueness and its
degrees are \eqref{rs:eq:spectralH} and \eqref{rs:eq:dq}, proved with
Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:cluster}; the passage listing the full coefficient
structure as unresolved is Part~II's research topic in
Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-conj17}, which now carries a note pointing here. That
topic also asked ``which of the printed degree and reciprocity claims
survive normalization'': both degree claims survive, and the printed
reciprocity sign survives except for $K\equiv1\pmod4$
(Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}, Section~\ref{sb:sec:printed-sign}).]

\subsection{Main theorem}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \IntZ=\{f\in\Q[n]: f(m)\in\Z\text{ for every }m\in\Z\}
 \label{sb:eq:IntZ}
\end{equation}
be the ring of integer-valued polynomials.

\begin{theorem}[Conjecture 17, proved and sign-corrected]
\label{sb:thm:main}
For every $K\ge1$ and every $0\le q\le K-1$, there is a unique polynomial
\[
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)\in\IntZ[t]
\]
such that, for all $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Ht_K(n;t)=\frac{1}{\Den_K(t)}
 \sum_{q=0}^{K-1}(-1)^q
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)t^{qn+\Btri_q}.}
 \label{sb:eq:main-expansion}
\end{equation}
It satisfies the exact bidegree formulas
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\deg_t\Cblock_{K,q}=\rdeg_{K,q},
 \qquad \deg_n\Cblock_{K,q}=\dq_{K,q}.}
 \label{sb:eq:bidegrees}
\end{equation}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Cblock_{K,K-1-q}(n,t)=\epsK_K
 t^{\rdeg_{K,q}}\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t^{-1}),}
 \label{sb:eq:corrected-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \epsK_K=(-1)^{K-1+a+ab}
 =\begin{cases}
   (-1)^{a-1},&K=2a,\\
   1,&K=2a+1.
  \end{cases}
 \label{sb:eq:epsilon}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $\epsK_K=-1$ exactly when $4\mid K$.
\end{theorem}

The proof occupies Sections~\ref{sb:sec:bialternant}--\ref{sb:sec:reciprocity}.
The integer-valued conclusion is stronger than mere rational polynomiality:
for every integer width $n\ge0$, each $\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)$ lies in $\Z[t]$.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: the name ``Conjecture 17,
proved and sign-corrected'' is the manuscript's. The relation
\eqref{sb:eq:corrected-reciprocity} with the sign \eqref{sb:eq:epsilon} is
not the relation printed in Cigler's equation~(83) when $K\equiv1\pmod4$
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:printed-sign}). This theorem therefore proves
Conjecture~17 in a corrected form; the conjecture as printed is false.]

\section{Confluent bialternants and spectral sectors}
\label{sb:sec:bialternant}

\subsection{A distinct-variable identity}
For distinct variables $x_1,\ldots,x_K$ and $A+C=K$, the rectangular
bialternant can be expanded along its last $A$ columns to give
\begin{equation}
 s_{(n^A)}(x_1,\ldots,x_K)
 =\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,K\}\\|I|=A}}
 \frac{\left(\prod_{i\in I}x_i\right)^{n+C}}
 {\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{j\notin I}(x_i-x_j)}.
 \label{sb:eq:subset-localization}
\end{equation}
For $A=1$, this is the usual partial-fraction formula for a complete
homogeneous symmetric polynomial.  In general it is the fixed-point or
Laplace form of the rectangular Schur character.  We will not take the
coalescing limit term by term; doing so would hide the cancellations that are
the substance of Conjecture~17.

For our specialization, $A=a$ and $C=b+1$.  The variables coalesce into the
three clusters
\begin{equation}
 (\xi_1,m_1)=(1,a),\qquad
 (\xi_2,m_2)=(t,1),\qquad
 (\xi_3,m_3)=(t^2,b).
 \label{sb:eq:clusters}
\end{equation}
The single middle cluster is decisive: it makes the spectral exponent
$q=r_2+2r_3$ determine the whole allocation.

\subsection{The normalized derivative matrix}
Let
\begin{equation}
 E_{K,n}=\{0,1,\ldots,b\}
 \cup\{n+b+1,n+b+2,\ldots,n+K-1\}.
 \label{sb:eq:exponents}
\end{equation}
The first block contains $b+1=C$ exponents and the second contains $a=A$.
For a cluster $i$ and an order $0\le h<m_i$, define the normalized derivative
row
\begin{equation}
 M_{(i,h),e}=\binom eh\xi_i^{e-h},
 \qquad e\in E_{K,n}.
 \label{sb:eq:confluent-matrix}
\end{equation}
This is $h!^{-1}(d/dx)^hx^e$ evaluated at $x=\xi_i$.  The confluent
bialternant reads
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_K(n;t)=\frac{\det M_{K,n}(t)}{\ConfDelta_K(t)},
 \label{sb:eq:confluent-bialternant}
\end{equation}
where, with the cluster order of \eqref{sb:eq:clusters},
\begin{align}
 \ConfDelta_K(t)
 &=\prod_{1\le i<j\le3}(\xi_j-\xi_i)^{m_im_j}\notag\\
 &=(t-1)^a(t^2-1)^{ab}(t^2-t)^b\notag\\
 &=t^b(t-1)^{a+b}(t^2-1)^{ab}.
 \label{sb:eq:confluent-vandermonde}
\end{align}
The normalized derivatives remove all hidden factorials from this formula.

\subsection{Laplace grouping and the unique allocation}
Expand $\det M_{K,n}(t)$ along the $a$ high-exponent columns.  If the selected
high rows contain $r_i$ rows from cluster $i$, then
\begin{equation}
 r_1+r_2+r_3=a,
 \qquad r_2\in\{0,1\}.
 \label{sb:eq:allocation-constraints}
\end{equation}
Every selected row from cluster $2$ contributes a factor $t^n$, and every
selected row from cluster $3$ contributes $t^{2n}$.  Hence its spectral
exponent is
\begin{equation}
 q=r_2+2r_3.
 \label{sb:eq:q-allocation}
\end{equation}
For each $0\le q\le K-1$, the allocation is unique:
\begin{equation}
 r_3=\left\lfloor\frac q2\right\rfloor,
 \qquad
 r_2=q-2\left\lfloor\frac q2\right\rfloor,
 \qquad
 r_1=a-\left\lceil\frac q2\right\rceil.
 \label{sb:eq:unique-allocation}
\end{equation}
Let $N_{K,q}(n,t)$ be the signed sum of all complementary-minor products with
this allocation.  Thus
\begin{equation}
 \det M_{K,n}(t)=\sum_{q=0}^{K-1}N_{K,q}(n,t),
 \label{sb:eq:N-sum}
\end{equation}
and comparison with \eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral} gives
\begin{equation}
 \Pblock_{K,q}(n;t)
 =\frac{N_{K,q}(n,t)}{\ConfDelta_K(t)t^{qn}}.
 \label{sb:eq:P-via-N}
\end{equation}
The candidate Conjecture~17 block is therefore forced to be
\begin{equation}
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)=(-1)^q\Den_K(t)t^{-\Btri_q}
 \Pblock_{K,q}(n;t).
 \label{sb:eq:C-definition}
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{sb:eq:confluent-vandermonde}, this becomes the much more revealing
formula
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)=
 (-1)^{q+a+ab}
 \frac{N_{K,q}(n,t)}
 {t^{qn+\Btri_q+b}(t-1)^b}.}
 \label{sb:eq:C-quotient}
\end{equation}
Consequently the entire polynomiality problem reduces to two divisibilities:
\begin{equation}
 t^{qn+\Btri_q+b}\mid N_{K,q}(n,t),
 \qquad
 (t-1)^b\mid N_{K,q}(n,t).
 \label{sb:eq:two-divisibilities}
\end{equation}
The first is a weighted assignment problem at $t=0$.  The second is a
collision problem for complementary jets at $t=1$.

\section{The two divisibility mechanisms}
\label{sb:sec:divisibility}

\subsection{Weighted assignments at the origin}
We begin with a general elementary bound.  Suppose a determinant has column
exponents
\[
 e_0<e_1<\cdots<e_{m-1}
\]
and rows of three types: weight zero, one middle row of weight one and
normalized derivative order zero, and $c$ weight-two rows with derivative
orders in a set $H$.  A nonzero permutation term assigns distinct exponents
to these rows.  The weight-two rows are cheapest on the $c$ smallest assigned
exponents, while the middle row, when present, is cheapest on the next one.
Therefore every determinant term has $t$-valuation at least
\begin{equation}
 2\sum_{j=0}^{c-1}e_j+\eta e_c-2\sum_{h\in H}h,
 \qquad \eta\in\{0,1\},
 \label{sb:eq:weighted-assignment-bound}
\end{equation}
where the middle term is omitted when $\eta=0$.  Cancellation can increase
this valuation but cannot decrease it.

\begin{lemma}[Exact triangular lower valuation]
\label{sb:lem:t-zero}
For every $K,n,q$,
\[
 \ord_{t=0}N_{K,q}(n,t)\ge qn+\Btri_q+b.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the derivative orders of the cluster-$3$ rows selected for the high
minor as $S$; the complementary orders form $T$.  Thus
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{h\in S}h+\sum_{h\in T}h=\frac{b(b-1)}2.
 \label{sb:eq:derivative-sum}
\end{equation}

First let $q=2h$.  The high minor contains $h$ cluster-$3$ rows and no middle
row.  Its columns start at $n+b+1$, so
\begin{equation}
 \ord_t(\text{high})\ge
 2h(n+b+1)+h(h-1)-2\sum_{r\in S}r.
 \label{sb:eq:even-high}
\end{equation}
The low minor contains $b-h$ cluster-$3$ rows and the middle row.  Applying
\eqref{sb:eq:weighted-assignment-bound} to the columns $0,1,\ldots,b$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \ord_t(\text{low})\ge
 (b-h)^2-2\sum_{r\in T}r.
 \label{sb:eq:even-low}
\end{equation}
Adding and using \eqref{sb:eq:derivative-sum},
\begin{align*}
 \ord_t(\text{high}\cdot\text{low})
 &\ge 2hn+2h^2+h+b\\
 &=qn+\binom{q+1}{2}+b.
\end{align*}

Now let $q=2h+1$.  The high minor contains $h$ cluster-$3$ rows and the
middle row.  The middle row receives the next high exponent after the $h$
weight-two assignments, so
\begin{align}
 \ord_t(\text{high})\ge{}&
 2h(n+b+1)+h(h-1)\notag\\
 &+(n+b+1+h)-2\sum_{r\in S}r.
 \label{sb:eq:odd-high}
\end{align}
The low minor contains $b-h$ cluster-$3$ rows and no middle row, whence
\begin{equation}
 \ord_t(\text{low})\ge
 (b-h)(b-h-1)-2\sum_{r\in T}r.
 \label{sb:eq:odd-low}
\end{equation}
The sum is
\[
 (2h+1)n+2h^2+3h+1+b
 =qn+\binom{q+1}{2}+b.
\]
Every Laplace product has the required valuation, so their signed sum does as
well.
\end{proof}

The appearance of $\binom{q+1}{2}$ is therefore not guessed from examples: it
is the exact tropical cost of assigning the vanishing nodes $t$ and $t^2$ to
the two complementary exponent blocks.

\subsection{A determinant collision lemma}
The cancellation of $(t-1)^b$ is subtler.  All three nodes tend to $1$, and
one must control each complementary minor, not only the full determinant.

For a finite multiset $H$ of nonnegative integers, define its collision cost
\begin{equation}
 \kappa(H)=\min\left\{
 \sum_i(p_i-h_i):
 p_i\ge h_i,\ p_i\in\N,\ p_i\text{ pairwise distinct}
 \right\}.
 \label{sb:eq:kappa}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Jet-collision valuation]
\label{sb:lem:jet-collision}
Let $f_1,\ldots,f_m$ be analytic near $1$.  For rows indexed by derivative
orders $h_i$ and analytic paths $x_i(u)=1+O(u)$, form
\[
 A_{ij}(u)=\frac{1}{h_i!}f_j^{(h_i)}(x_i(u)).
\]
Then
\[
 \ord_{u=0}\det A(u)\ge\kappa(\{h_1,\ldots,h_m\}).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $D^h=f^{(h)}/h!$.  Taylor's formula gives
\begin{equation}
 D^{h_i}f_j(x_i(u))
 =\sum_{r\ge0}\binom{h_i+r}{h_i}
 D^{h_i+r}f_j(1)\,(x_i(u)-1)^r.
 \label{sb:eq:jet-taylor}
\end{equation}
A multilinear determinant term choosing jet order $p_i=h_i+r_i$ from row
$i$ vanishes unless the $p_i$ are distinct, because equal jet orders give
identical row vectors.  Since $x_i(u)-1=O(u)$, that term has order at least
$\sum_i(p_i-h_i)$.  Minimizing gives \eqref{sb:eq:kappa}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Two complementary jet tables]
\label{sb:lem:two-server}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak H_{a,b}
 =\{0,1,\ldots,a-1\}\sqcup\{0\}\sqcup\{0,1,\ldots,b-1\}.
 \label{sb:eq:full-derivative-multiset}
\end{equation}
For every partition $\mathfrak H_{a,b}=H_1\sqcup H_2$,
\[
 \kappa(H_1)+\kappa(H_2)\ge b.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Interpret the two sets as jobs assigned to two single-server schedules.  A
job released at time $h$ is scheduled at the distinct integer time $p\ge h$;
its waiting time is $p-h$.  For every $0\le r<b$, the full multiset contains
\[
 (r+1)+1+(r+1)=2r+3
\]
jobs released by time $r$.  The two servers have only $2(r+1)=2r+2$ slots
through time $r$.  Hence at least one job released by time $r$ must be
scheduled after $r$.

For any schedule,
\[
 \sum_i(p_i-h_i)=
 \sum_{r\ge0}\#\{i:h_i\le r<p_i\}.
\]
The preceding deficit contributes at least one for each
$r=0,1,\ldots,b-1$, proving the lower bound $b$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Universal missing-factor cancellation]
\label{sb:lem:t-one}
For every $K,n,q$,
\[
 (t-1)^b\mid N_{K,q}(n,t).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $u=t-1$.  The three node paths are
\[
 1,\qquad 1+u,\qquad (1+u)^2=1+2u+u^2.
\]
Consider one Laplace product contributing to $N_{K,q}$.  Its high and low
minors partition the complete derivative multiset
$\mathfrak H_{a,b}$.  By Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:jet-collision}, their valuations are at
least $\kappa(H_1)$ and $\kappa(H_2)$.  By Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:two-server}, the product
has $u$-valuation at least $b$.  This holds term by term, hence also for the
signed grouped sum.
\end{proof}

The proof exposes why the missing factor is exactly associated with the
single intermediate node.  The full confluent Vandermonde contains
$(t^2-t)^b=t^b(t-1)^b$, but Cigler's proposed denominator omits the
$(t-1)^b$ part.  Complementary jets supply it automatically.

\subsection{Polynomiality and integer values}

\begin{corollary}[Polynomial blocks]
\label{sb:cor:polynomiality}
The quotient in \eqref{sb:eq:C-quotient} belongs to $\Q[n,t]$.  For every
integer $n\ge0$, it belongs to $\Z[t]$; consequently
$\Cblock_{K,q}\in\IntZ[t]$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The high-block rows contribute the common factor $t^{qn}$ for the allocation
$q$.  After it is removed, every matrix entry is polynomial in $t$ and in the
binomial polynomials $\binom{n+c}{h}$, hence lies in $\Q[n,t]$ and is
integer-valued in $n$.  Lemmas~\ref{sb:lem:t-zero} and~\ref{sb:lem:t-one} prove
exact divisibility by the monic polynomial
$t^{qn+\Btri_q+b}(t-1)^b$ for each nonnegative integer $n$.  Division by a
monic polynomial preserves integral coefficients.  The quotient is
polynomial in $n$, and its coefficient polynomials take integer values at
all nonnegative integers; finite-difference interpolation places them in
$\IntZ$.
\end{proof}

Substitution of \eqref{sb:eq:C-definition} into
\eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral} now proves the existence part of
Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}.  Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the spectral
blocks $\Pblock_{K,q}$.

\section{Exact width degree and leading coefficient}
\label{sb:sec:leading}

\subsection{The inherited confluent leading term}
The repository's general cluster theorem gives the leading coefficient in
$n$ of each canonical spectral polynomial.  We recall the finite formula in
the notation needed here.  For cluster multiplicities $m_i$, selected counts
$r_i$, complements $s_i=m_i-r_i$, total high size $A$, and $C=K-A$, define
\begin{equation}
 \beta(m,r)=
 \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}j!}
 {\displaystyle\prod_{j=m-r}^{m-1}j!},
 \qquad \beta(m,0)=\beta(m,m)=1.
 \label{sb:eq:beta}
\end{equation}
The degree is $d=\sum_i r_is_i$, and the leading coefficient of the spectral
polynomial is
\begin{equation}
 L_{\boldsymbol r}=(-1)^{\sum_{i<j}r_is_j}
 \frac{\displaystyle\prod_i
 \beta(m_i,r_i)\xi_i^{Cr_i-r_is_i}}
 {\displaystyle\prod_{i<j}
 (\xi_j-\xi_i)^{s_ir_j+r_is_j}}.
 \label{sb:eq:cluster-leading}
\end{equation}
It is nonzero whenever the cluster nodes are distinct.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: ``the repository's general
cluster theorem'' is Part~II's Theorem~\ref{rs:thm:cluster}. Formula
\eqref{sb:eq:cluster-leading} is \eqref{rs:eq:LC} symbol for symbol, with
$\beta(m,r)$ for Part~II's $B(m,r)$ \eqref{rs:eq:Bfactor}; the degree
$d=\sum_ir_is_i$ is Part~II's $d_{\boldsymbol r}$ \eqref{rs:eq:lambdad}.]

\subsection{Evaluation for even sectors}
Let $q=2h$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \boldsymbol r=(a-h,0,h),
 \qquad
 \boldsymbol s=(h,1,b-h),
 \label{sb:eq:even-allocation}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \dq_{K,2h}=h(a+b-2h).
 \label{sb:eq:even-d}
\end{equation}
Direct substitution into \eqref{sb:eq:cluster-leading} gives
\begin{equation}
 [n^{\dq_{K,2h}}]\Pblock_{K,2h}
 =(-1)^{(a-h)(b+1-h)}
 \beta(a,a-h)\beta(b,h)
 \frac{t^{\Btri_{2h}}}
 {(t-1)^a(t^2-1)^{ab-\dq_{K,2h}}}.
 \label{sb:eq:P-leading-even}
\end{equation}
Multiplying by $\Den_K(t)t^{-\Btri_{2h}}$ and simplifying the parity gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 [n^{\dq_{K,2h}}]\Cblock_{K,2h}
 =\beta(a,a-h)\beta(b,h)
 (1-t^2)^{\dq_{K,2h}}.}
 \label{sb:eq:C-leading-even}
\end{equation}
The sign disappears because the total exponent reduces to $h(h+1)$ modulo
two.

\subsection{Evaluation for odd sectors}
Let $q=2h+1$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \boldsymbol r=(a-h-1,1,h),
 \qquad
 \boldsymbol s=(h+1,0,b-h),
 \label{sb:eq:odd-allocation}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \dq_{K,2h+1}=(a-h-1)(h+1)+h(b-h).
 \label{sb:eq:odd-d}
\end{equation}
Put
\begin{equation}
 \delta_K=b+1-a=
 \begin{cases}0,&K\text{ even},\\1,&K\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{sb:eq:delta-parity}
\end{equation}
The cluster formula becomes
\begin{align}
 [n^{\dq_{K,2h+1}}]\Pblock_{K,2h+1}
 ={}&(-1)^{(a-h)(b-h)}
 \beta(a,a-h-1)\beta(b,h)\notag\\
 &\times
 \frac{t^{\Btri_{2h+1}}}
 {(t-1)^{b+1}(t^2-1)^{ab-\dq_{K,2h+1}-\delta_K}}.
 \label{sb:eq:P-leading-odd}
\end{align}
After applying \eqref{sb:eq:C-definition}, the excess
$(t^2-1)^{\delta_K}/(t-1)^{\delta_K}$ is $1$ for even $K$ and $1+t$ for odd
$K$.  The remaining sign reduces to $(-1)^h$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 [n^{\dq_{K,2h+1}}]\Cblock_{K,2h+1}
 =(-1)^h\beta(a,a-h-1)\beta(b,h)
 \chi_K(t)(1-t^2)^{\dq_{K,2h+1}},}
 \label{sb:eq:C-leading-odd}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \chi_K(t)=\begin{cases}1,&K\text{ even},\\1+t,&K\text{ odd}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{sb:eq:chi}
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Exact degree in $n$ and a nonzero constant term]
\label{sb:cor:n-degree}
For all $K,q$,
\[
 \deg_n\Cblock_{K,q}=\dq_{K,q}.
\]
Moreover the coefficient of $t^0$ in
$[n^{\dq_{K,q}}]\Cblock_{K,q}$ is nonzero.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The multiplication in \eqref{sb:eq:C-definition} is independent of $n$, so the
nonzero degree $\dq_{K,q}$ from \eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral} is preserved.
Equations~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even} and~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd} have
nonzero constant term $\pm\beta(\cdot,\cdot)\beta(\cdot,\cdot)$.
\end{proof}

The leading formulas are useful independently of Conjecture~17.  They give a
closed product expression for the highest-width coefficient of every block,
and they certify that none of the origin valuations used in
Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:t-zero} is an artifact of total cancellation.

\section{Reciprocity, exact \texorpdfstring{$t$}{t}-degree, and the sign correction}
\label{sb:sec:reciprocity}

\subsection{Schur reciprocity}
The specialized rectangular Schur polynomial is reciprocal:
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_K(n;t^{-1})=t^{-(K-1)n}\Ht_K(n;t).
 \label{sb:eq:H-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
For odd $K$, this follows because inversion of the alphabet, followed by
multiplication by $t^2$, permutes its entries.  For even $K$, it is the
rectangular complement identity; the product of the alphabet entries is
$t^{K-1}$.  Comparing the unique decompositions
\eqref{sb:eq:existing-spectral} on both sides gives
\begin{equation}
 \Pblock_{K,K-1-q}(n;t)=\Pblock_{K,q}(n;t^{-1}).
 \label{sb:eq:P-reciprocity}
\end{equation}

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70:
\eqref{sb:eq:H-reciprocity} is the reciprocity \eqref{eq:pal} of Part~I's
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, proved there from the parity factorization; the
Schur argument above is a second route. The uniqueness used for
\eqref{sb:eq:P-reciprocity} is that of Part~II's \eqref{rs:eq:spectralH}.]

The denominator transforms as
\begin{equation}
 \Den_K(t^{-1})=(-1)^{a+ab}t^{-a-2ab}\Den_K(t).
 \label{sb:eq:D-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
Let $q'=K-1-q$.  Two elementary identities, using $K=a+b+1$ and
$a-b\in\{0,1\}$, are
\begin{align}
 a+2ab-\Btri_q-\Btri_{q'}&=qq'=\rdeg_{K,q},
 \label{sb:eq:power-identity}\\
 (-1)^{q'-q+a+ab}&=(-1)^{K-1+a+ab}=\epsK_K.
 \label{sb:eq:sign-identity}
\end{align}
Substituting \eqref{sb:eq:P-reciprocity} into \eqref{sb:eq:C-definition} now proves
\eqref{sb:eq:corrected-reciprocity}.

\subsection{Exact degree in \texorpdfstring{$t$}{t}}
Polynomiality alone gives no sharp upper degree.  Reciprocity supplies it
without another determinant estimate.

\begin{proposition}[Exact spectral-block width]
\label{sb:prop:t-degree}
For every $K,q$,
\[
 \deg_t\Cblock_{K,q}=\rdeg_{K,q}=q(K-1-q).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:n-degree}, the constant coefficient in $t$ of
$\Cblock_{K,q}$ is not the zero polynomial in $n$.  The same holds for the
paired block $q'=K-1-q$.  In
\[
 \Cblock_{K,q'}(n,t)=\epsK_K t^{\rdeg_{K,q}}
 \Cblock_{K,q}(n,t^{-1}),
\]
the nonzero constant term of the left side is the image of the top
$t$-coefficient of the right-side partner.  Thus the latter occurs exactly
at degree $\rdeg_{K,q}$.  Symmetry gives the statement for both blocks.
\end{proof}

This proves both degree assertions in Conjecture~17 and completes the proof
of Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main}.

\subsection{Where the printed sign fails}
\label{sb:sec:printed-sign}
Cigler's equation~(83) prints the sign
\begin{equation}
 (-1)^{\lfloor K/2\rfloor-1}
 \label{sb:eq:printed-sign}
\end{equation}
for all shifts.  For even $K=2a$, this equals the correct sign
$(-1)^{a-1}$.  For odd $K=2a+1$, however, the correct sign is always $+1$.
The printed sign therefore fails exactly when $K\equiv1\pmod4$ and $K\ge5$.

The first failure is already visible inside the source.  Its displayed
central block for $K=5$ is
\begin{align}
 \Cblock_{5,2}(n,t)={}&(n+2)(n+3)-t
 -2(n+2)(n+3)t^2-t^3\notag\\
 &+(n+2)(n+3)t^4.
 \label{sb:eq:c52}
\end{align}
It satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \Cblock_{5,2}(n,t)=t^4\Cblock_{5,2}(n,t^{-1}),
 \label{sb:eq:c52-palindromic}
\end{equation}
whereas the printed sign would require its negative.  An anti-reciprocal
polynomial of even degree would in particular have zero central coefficient;
the coefficient of $t^2$ in \eqref{sb:eq:c52} is nonzero.  Thus this is not a
matter of convention or normalization.  The correction is forced before any
new examples are computed.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: two remarks of this merge.
First, the residue class $K\equiv1\pmod4$ also contains $K=1$. There the
printed sign is $(-1)^{\lfloor1/2\rfloor-1}=-1$, so it would demand
$\Cblock_{1,0}=-\Cblock_{1,0}$, whereas $\Cblock_{1,0}=1$
(Corollary~\ref{sb:cor:boundary}): it fails there too, trivially. The
manuscript's range ``$K\ge5$'' is exact if Cigler's Conjecture~17 is stated
only for $K\ge2$; whether it is was not re-checked against his paper during
this intake, so the safe reading is that the printed sign fails for every
$K\equiv1\pmod4$ to which the conjecture applies. Second, an independent
computation during the intake (blocks read off by separating $t$-degrees at
large widths and interpolating in~$n$, from the Jacobi--Trudi form, bridged
to the raw Hankel determinant for $K\le5$, $n\le5$) confirmed the
bidegrees, the integer values, $\epsK_K$ and the reconstruction of
$\Ht_K(n;t)$ at small widths for $K\le7$, confirmed $\Cblock_{5,2}$ as
displayed in \eqref{sb:eq:c52}, and found the printed sign failing at $K=1$
and at $K=5$ for every $q$ and holding at $K=2,3,4,6,7$. It is not shipped.
Part~II's correction of Cigler's equation~(85) concerns a different
equation and the residue class $4\mid k$
(Section~\ref{sb:sec:merge-provenance}).]

\begin{corollary}[Boundary blocks]
\label{sb:cor:boundary}
For every $K\ge1$,
\[
 \Cblock_{K,0}(n,t)=1,
 \qquad
 \Cblock_{K,K-1}(n,t)=\epsK_K.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Both blocks have bidegree $(0,0)$.  Equation~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even} gives
the first value, and reciprocity gives the second.
\end{proof}

\section{Examples and consistency checks}
\label{sb:sec:examples}

\subsection{The first three shifts}
For $K=1$, the only block is $\Cblock_{1,0}=1$.  For $K=2$,
\[
 \Cblock_{2,0}=\Cblock_{2,1}=1,
 \qquad
 \Ht_2(n;t)=\frac{1-t^{n+1}}{1-t}.
\]
For $K=3$,
\[
 \Cblock_{3,0}=1,
 \qquad \Cblock_{3,1}=1+t,
 \qquad \Cblock_{3,2}=1,
\]
and
\begin{equation}
 \Ht_3(n;t)=
 \frac{1-(1+t)t^{n+1}+t^{2n+3}}
 {(1-t)(1-t^2)}.
 \label{sb:eq:K3}
\end{equation}
The central block has $t$-degree one and $n$-degree zero, as prescribed by
\eqref{sb:eq:bidegrees}.

\subsection{Shift four}
Here $a=2$, $b=1$, and $\epsK_4=-1$.  The blocks are
\begin{align}
 \Cblock_{4,0}&=1,\notag\\
 \Cblock_{4,1}&=n+2+t-(n+2)t^2,\notag\\
 \Cblock_{4,2}&=n+2-t-(n+2)t^2,\notag\\
 \Cblock_{4,3}&=-1.
 \label{sb:eq:K4-blocks}
\end{align}
They obey
\[
 \Cblock_{4,2}(n,t)=-t^2\Cblock_{4,1}(n,t^{-1}),
 \qquad \Cblock_{4,3}=-\Cblock_{4,0}.
\]
Substitution gives
\begin{align}
 \Ht_4(n;t)=\frac{1}{(1-t)^2(1-t^2)^2}\bigl(&1
 -\Cblock_{4,1}t^{n+1}
 +\Cblock_{4,2}t^{2n+3}\notag\\
 &-\Cblock_{4,3}t^{3n+6}\bigr).
 \label{sb:eq:K4-expansion}
\end{align}
The leading $n$-coefficients are $1-t^2$ and $1-t^2$, in agreement with
\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd} and \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even}.

\subsection{Shift five and the corrected central symmetry}
Here $a=b=2$ and all paired signs are positive.  One finds
\begin{align}
 \Cblock_{5,0}&=1,\notag\\
 \Cblock_{5,1}&=(1-t^2)\bigl(n+2+(n+3)t\bigr),\notag\\
 \Cblock_{5,2}&=(n+2)(n+3)-t
 -2(n+2)(n+3)t^2-t^3\notag\\
 &\hspace{7em}+(n+2)(n+3)t^4,\notag\\
 \Cblock_{5,3}&=t^3\Cblock_{5,1}(n,t^{-1}),\notag\\
 \Cblock_{5,4}&=1.
 \label{sb:eq:K5-blocks}
\end{align}
The $n$-leading terms are
\begin{align*}
 [n]\Cblock_{5,1}&=(1+t)(1-t^2),\\
 [n^2]\Cblock_{5,2}&=(1-t^2)^2,\\
 [n]\Cblock_{5,3}&=-(1+t)(1-t^2).
\end{align*}
The sign change between the leading coefficients of the odd sectors comes
from $(-1)^h$ in \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd}, not from reciprocal pairing.

\subsection{Degree and sign table}
For orientation, the first shifts have the following data.  The maximum
$n$-degree is $\max_q\dq_{K,q}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}crrcc@{}}
\toprule
$K$ & $a$ & $b$ & $\epsK_K$ & $\max_q\deg_n\Cblock_{K,q}$\\
\midrule
1&0&0&$+1$&0\\
2&1&0&$+1$&0\\
3&1&1&$+1$&0\\
4&2&1&$-1$&1\\
5&2&2&$+1$&2\\
6&3&2&$+1$&3\\
7&3&3&$+1$&4\\
8&4&3&$-1$&6\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Negative reciprocity occurs at $K=4,8,12,\ldots$, not at odd shifts.

\section{Exact computation and verification}
\label{sb:sec:verification}

\subsection{A finite algorithm}
The proof gives a direct exact algorithm that avoids an $n\times n$ Hankel
determinant.

\begin{algorithm}[Confluent spectral blocks]
\label{sb:alg:blocks}
Given $K$ and a nonnegative integer $n$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute $a,b$ and the exponent list \eqref{sb:eq:exponents}.
\item Build the $K\times K$ normalized derivative matrix
      \eqref{sb:eq:confluent-matrix} over $\Z[t]$.
\item Expand along the $a$ high columns and group each complementary-minor
      product by $q=r_2+2r_3$.
\item Divide the grouped numerator by
      $t^{qn+\Btri_q+b}(t-1)^b$ and apply the sign
      $(-1)^{q+a+ab}$.
\end{enumerate}
The result is $\Cblock_{K,q}(n,t)$.  To recover the full polynomial in $n$,
interpolate from $\dq_{K,q}+1$ integer widths.
\end{algorithm}

The divisions in Step~4 are certified by the two lemmas, not inferred from a
computer algebra simplification.  The determinant size depends only on the
shift $K$, whereas the original Hankel matrix has size $n$.

\subsection{What the shipped verifier checks}
The package includes \path{code/verify.py}, using exact SymPy arithmetic.  A
recorded run with Python~3.13 and SymPy~1.14 performs the following checks.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}XrX@{}}
\toprule
Check & Cases & Range or meaning\\
\midrule
Polynomial bidegrees & 36 & Every block for $1\le K\le8$\\
Corrected reciprocity & 36 & Every paired block for $1\le K\le8$\\
Explicit $n$-leading coefficient & 36 & Equations~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even} and \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd}\\
Schur reconstruction & 40 & Interpolation widths and two fresh widths for each $K\le8$\\
Integral $t$-coefficients & 216 & Every tested block at every tested integer width\\
Original Hankel--Schur bridge & 36 & $1\le K\le6$, $0\le n\le5$\\
Printed-sign counterexample & 1 & $K=5,q=2$: printed relation nonzero, corrected relation zero\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The verifier constructs the original moments \eqref{sb:eq:moments} independently,
forms their Hankel determinant, and compares it with the Schur side before
checking the spectral decomposition.  It therefore exercises every bridge in
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{moments}\longrightarrow\text{Hankel determinant}
 \longrightarrow\text{Schur polynomial}\\
 \longrightarrow\text{confluent sectors}
 \longrightarrow\text{Conjecture 17 blocks}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
All computations are exact.  The finite range does not prove the general
theorem; its purpose is to catch indexing, sign, exponent, and normalization
errors in the written derivation.

\subsection{Complexity remarks}
For fixed $K$, each matrix entry is a binomial polynomial in $n$ times a
monomial in $t$.  There are $\binom Ka$ Laplace selections.  A direct symbolic
implementation is therefore exponential in $K$ but independent of the width
as a matrix dimension.  Several improvements are immediate:
\begin{itemize}
\item compute each allocation with Cauchy--Binet rather than enumerate every
      row subset;
\item use fraction-free elimination in the integer-valued polynomial basis
      $\binom nr$;
\item exploit reciprocal pairing to compute only half the sectors;
\item use the leading formulas as degree and normalization certificates;
\item reinterpret grouped minors as subresultants, which should provide a
      polynomial-time algorithm in $K$ for one sector.
\end{itemize}
No bit-complexity claim is made for the reference implementation.

\section{A formalization route}
\label{sb:sec:formalization}
The proof is well suited to a staged Lean formalization because its difficult
steps are finite algebra and discrete valuation estimates.

\subsection{Layer 1: the finite Schur bridge}
Represent the exponent list, normalized derivative rows, and confluent
Vandermonde as finite matrices over $\Q[n,t]$.  The repository already
contains determinant and Schur infrastructure around this family.  The new
formal target is the grouped Laplace identity \eqref{sb:eq:P-via-N}, including
the unique allocation \eqref{sb:eq:unique-allocation}.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: the ``determinant and Schur
infrastructure'' is the paper proofs of Parts~I--III and their Python
verifiers. No Lean or Rocq declaration in ProveIt concerns Cigler's
polynomials; this section is a proposed route, and nothing in it is
implemented.]

\subsection{Layer 2: valuation certificates}
Formalize a valuation lower bound for determinant permutation terms over
$\Q[t]$.  The origin argument reduces to rearrangement on a finite list of
column exponents.  It is important to state the result as a lower bound;
minimal monomials may cancel, but cancellation cannot create a lower
valuation.

For $t=1$, define $\kappa(H)$ by \eqref{sb:eq:kappa}.  The Taylor-jet proof can
be implemented either with truncated power series or with divisibility by
powers of $t-1$.  The two-server lemma is a finite Hall-type counting
argument.  Its cumulative form, $2r+3>2r+2$ for $r<b$, avoids any scheduling
library.

\subsection{Layer 3: integer-valued polynomial blocks}
Once the two divisibilities are formalized, define $\Cblock_{K,q}$ by exact
polynomial division.  The ring $\IntZ$ can initially be avoided by proving
that every natural-number specialization lies in $\Z[t]$.  A later layer can
identify the coefficients in the binomial basis and package the result as an
integer-valued polynomial theorem.

\subsection{Layer 4: leading terms and reciprocity}
The existing confluent leading-coefficient theorem can be specialized to the
two allocations \eqref{sb:eq:even-allocation} and
\eqref{sb:eq:odd-allocation}.  The remaining work is factorial-product
simplification and parity arithmetic.  Schur reciprocity then proves
\eqref{sb:eq:corrected-reciprocity}; exact $t$-degree follows from the nonzero
constant terms.  This order avoids relying on a computer-generated degree
bound.

\section{Further research questions and topics}
\label{sb:sec:further}
The theorem settles polynomiality, bidegrees, reciprocity, and leading width
terms.  It also opens several more structural questions.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: relation to the questions of
Parts~II and~III. Sections~\ref{sb:q:coefficients} and~\ref{sb:q:sign}
continue Part~II's topic in Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-conj17}, which
Theorem~\ref{sb:thm:main} answers in corrected form; they ask for finer
structure. Section~\ref{sb:q:zeros} is related to Part~III's
Section~\ref{cp:q:numerators}, which concerns numerators at cyclotomic
parameters. Section~\ref{sb:q:parameters} uses the alphabet of Part~II's
topic ``A two-parameter Hankel deformation'' but asks for sector
denominators, not for a moment family. Sections~\ref{sb:q:clusters}
and~\ref{sb:q:certified} continue Part~II's
Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-noncollision} and Part~III's
Sections~\ref{cp:q:middle} and~\ref{cp:q:certified}.
Section~\ref{sb:q:merger} is Part~II's topic ``A uniform crossover as the
poles merge'', now with the block formula as input. Section~\ref{sb:q:roots}
is answered by Part~III (see the note there). Section~\ref{sb:q:subresultant}
is new.]

\subsection{A closed determinant or subresultant formula}
\label{sb:q:subresultant}
The grouped numerators are sums of products of complementary confluent
minors.  Their universal $(t-1)^b$ factor strongly suggests a subresultant or
Sylvester-double-sum interpretation.  Find a single determinant for
$\Cblock_{K,q}$ whose polynomiality is manifest and whose size is governed by
$\dq_{K,q}$ rather than by $K$.  Such a formula could turn the current
Laplace algorithm into a substantially faster certified algorithm.

\subsection{The full coefficient structure in \texorpdfstring{$n$}{n}}
\label{sb:q:coefficients}
Equations~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even} and~\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd} determine only
the top coefficient in $n$.  Determine every coefficient in the binomial
basis $\binom nr$.  The computations suggest product factorizations at
$t=0$ and unexpectedly sparse formulas near the top degree.  A satisfactory
answer should explain integer-valuedness directly and identify the relevant
flagged tableaux, nonintersecting paths, or equivariant multiplicities.

\subsection{Sign regularity and positivity bases}
\label{sb:q:sign}
The ordinary $t$-coefficients have mixed signs, so naive positivity is false.
Are the blocks sign-regular after expansion in a basis adapted to reciprocity,
such as
\[
 t^j+\epsK_K t^{\rdeg_{K,q}-j},
 \qquad
 (1-t^2)^r(1+t)^s,
\]
or after expressing the $n$ dependence in the binomial basis?  The explicit
leading term is sign-controlled; classify how far that control extends to
lower width degrees.

\subsection{Zeros and reciprocal geometry}
\label{sb:q:zeros}
Reciprocity pairs the nonzero zeros of opposite sectors by inversion.  Study
whether suitably normalized blocks have all real, unit-circle, or
interlacing zeros in any nontrivial range of $K,q,n$.  The $K=4$ and $K=5$
examples already show that a universal real-rootedness statement would need
qualification.  Stable-polynomial or total-positivity methods may nevertheless
apply to special sectors.

\subsection{Independent middle and outer parameters}
\label{sb:q:parameters}
Replace the alphabet by
\[
 (1^a,u,v^b).
\]
The present curve is $u=t$, $v=t^2$.  Determine the exact grouped-sector
denominators in $u,v$, then characterize algebraic curves $(u(t),v(t))$ on
which an analogue of the jet-collision cancellation occurs.  The proof here
shows that the path speeds $0,1,2$ create a deficit of exactly $b$; other speed
patterns should lead to a tropical scheduling invariant.

\subsection{More clusters and a capacity theorem}
\label{sb:q:clusters}
For a general repeated alphabet
$(\xi_1^{m_1},\ldots,\xi_s^{m_s})$, a Laplace term partitions the derivative
jobs among two complementary minors.  Generalize Lemma~\ref{sb:lem:two-server} to a
capacity theorem for several colliding paths and prescribed speed vectors.
This should produce automatic denominator certificates for broad families of
rectangular Schur specializations.

\subsection{Roots of unity and reduced recurrences}
\label{sb:q:roots}
The generic roots $t^q$ collide at roots of unity.  The repository already
identifies this as an open recurrence problem.  The new polynomial blocks
make cancellation at a collision explicit: sum the paired
$\Pblock_{K,q}$ over a residue class and determine its surviving degree.
A closed formula would classify every reduced recurrence at every complex
root of unity.

\noindent[Added 30 September 2026, batch~70: answered. ``The repository
already identifies this as an open recurrence problem'' was true at the pin
(Part~II's Section~\ref{cp:sec:rs-cyclotomic}). Part~III, added afterwards,
classifies the reduced denominators and minimal recurrences at every root
of unity (Theorem~\ref{cp:thm:main}) and at every complex parameter
(Corollary~\ref{cp:cor:complex}), by summing the canonical polynomials
$P_{k,q}=\Pblock_{K,q}$ over a residue class as suggested here. It does not
use the blocks $\Cblock_{K,q}$; a derivation of its classification from the
block formula has not been written.]

\subsection{The pole-merger scaling \texorpdfstring{$t=e^{-s/n}$}{t = exp(-s/n)}}
\label{sb:q:merger}
For fixed $0<t<1$, one spectral root dominates; at $t=1$, all roots merge and
the Schur value becomes a polynomial in $n$.  Insert the proven block formula
into the critical scaling $t=e^{-s/n}$ and derive a uniform all-orders
expansion.  The exact bidegrees make this a finite cancellation problem for
each fixed $K$, while the leading formulas should determine the first
nontrivial profile.

\subsection{Machine-checked proof and certified extraction}
\label{sb:q:certified}
Formalize the divisibility lemmas and build an extractor that outputs
$\Cblock_{K,q}$ together with certificates of its two exact divisions,
bidegrees, and reciprocal partner.  The output should be proof-producing,
not merely a symbolic expression whose cancellations are trusted to a CAS.

\section{Conclusion}
Cigler's Conjecture~17 is governed by two degenerations of one confluent
bialternant: a weighted assignment at $t=0$ creates the triangular exponent,
and a two-table jet collision at $t=1$ cancels the missing denominator factor.
Together with the rectangular Schur identity and cluster theorem in
\texttt{ProveIt}, these mechanisms give polynomial blocks, exact bidegrees,
and closed leading terms.  Schur reciprocity fixes the printed sign error:
every odd shift is positively reciprocal.

The resulting circuit is exact and finite:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{Cigler moments}
 \longrightarrow \text{Hankel determinant}
 \longrightarrow \text{rectangular Schur character}\\
 \longrightarrow \text{confluent spectral sectors}
 \longrightarrow \text{integer-valued polynomial blocks}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The remaining problems concern finer factorization, positivity, root geometry,
collision specializations, asymptotic pole mergers, and formal certification,
not the existence or the degree of the Conjecture~17 polynomials.

\clearpage
\renewcommand{\thesection}{\Alph{section}}
\renewcommand{\theHsection}{sb.app.\Alph{section}}
\setcounter{section}{7}
\section{Algebra behind the leading terms}
\label{sb:app:leading-algebra}
For completeness, this appendix records the exponent reductions used in
Section~\ref{sb:sec:leading}.

For $q=2h$, the exponent of $t$ in \eqref{sb:eq:cluster-leading} is
\[
 2h\bigl((b+1)-(b-h)\bigr)-h
 =2h(h+1)-h=2h^2+h=\Btri_{2h},
\]
where the subtraction by $h$ comes from
$(t^2-t)^h=t^h(t-1)^h$.  The exponent of $(t^2-1)$ in the denominator is
\[
 h^2+(a-h)(b-h)=ab-h(a+b-2h)=ab-\dq_{K,2h}.
\]
After replacing $(t^2-1)^d$ by $(-1)^d(1-t^2)^d$, the total sign exponent is
\[
 (a-h)(b+1-h)+a+ab+\dq_{K,2h}
 \equiv h(h+1)\equiv0\pmod2.
\]

For $q=2h+1$, the $t$-exponent is
\[
 (b+1)+2h(h+1)-(b-h)=2h^2+3h+1=\Btri_{2h+1}.
\]
The cross exponent satisfies
\[
 ab-\left(h(h+1)+(a-h-1)(b-h)\right)
 =\dq_{K,2h+1}+b+1-a.
\]
The residual quotient is therefore $(1+t)^{b+1-a}(1-t^2)^d$.  The sign
exponent reduces to
\[
 1+(a-h)(b-h)+a+ab+d\equiv h\pmod2.
\]
These are precisely \eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-even} and
\eqref{sb:eq:C-leading-odd}.

\section{Package contents and reproduction}
\label{sb:app:package}
The archive contains:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{article.tex} and the compiled \texttt{article.pdf};
\item \texttt{code/verify.py};
\item \texttt{data/verification.json} and \texttt{data/verification.log};
\item \texttt{README.md}, \texttt{STATUS.md}, \texttt{SOURCES.md},
      \texttt{requirements.txt}, and a \texttt{Makefile}.
\end{itemize}
To reproduce the exact checks and the PDF, run
\begin{verbatim}
python -m pip install -r requirements.txt
python code/verify.py --output data/verification.json
python /home/oai/skills/pdfs/scripts/latex_to_pdf.py \
  article.tex -o article.pdf
\end{verbatim}
The recorded verifier run completed all checks.  Runtime is environment
dependent and is not a complexity claim.
